Sharp quantitative stability of Struwe’s decomposition of the Poincaré-Sobolev inequalities on the hyperbolic space: Part I
Mousomi Bhakta, Debdip Ganguly, Debabrata Karmakar, Saikat Mazumdar

Abstract
. A classical result owing to Mancini and Sandeep [ 41 ] asserts that all positive solutions of the Poincaré-Sobolev equation on the hyperbolic space − Δ 𝔹 n ​ u − λ ​ u = | u | p − 1 ​ u , u ∈ H 1 ​ ( 𝔹 n ) , -\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u\,-\,\lambda u\,=\,|u|^{p-1}u,\quad u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}), are unique up to hyperbolic isometries where n ≥ 3 , n\geq 3, 1 < p ≤ n + 2 n − 2 1<p\leq\frac{n+2}{n-2} and λ ≤ ( n − 1 ) 2 4 . \lambda\leq\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}. In the spirit of Struwe, Bhakta-Sandeep [ 6 ] proved the following non-quantitative stability result: if u m ≥ 0 , u_{m}\geq 0, and ‖ Δ 𝔹 n ​ u m + λ ​ u m + u m p ‖ H − 1 → 0 , \|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u_{m}+\lambda u_{m}+u_{m}^{p}\|_{H^{-1}}\rightarrow 0, then δ ⁡ ( u m ) := dist ​ ( u m , ℳ λ ) → 0 , \delta(u_{m}):=\mbox{dist}(u_{m},\mathcal{M_{\lambda}})\rightarrow 0, where dist ​ ( u m , ℳ λ ) \mbox{dist}(u_{m},\mathcal{M_{\lambda}}) denotes the H 1 H^{1} -distance of u m u_{m} from the manifold of sums of superpositions of hyperbolic bubbles and (localized) Aubin-Talenti bubbles. In this article, we study the quantitative stability of Struwe decomposition. We prove under certain bounds on ‖ ∇ u ‖ L 2 ​ ( 𝔹 n ) \|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n})} the inequality δ ⁡ ( u ) ≲ ‖ Δ 𝔹 n ​ u + λ ​ u + u p ‖ H − 1 , \delta(u)\lesssim\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+u^{p}\|_{H^{-1}}, holds whenever p > 2 p>2 and hence forcing the dimensional restriction 3 ≤ n ≤ 5 . 3\leq n\leq 5. Moreover, it fails for any n ≥ 3 n\geq 3 and p ∈ ( 1 , 2 ] . p\in(1,2]. This strengthens the phenomenon observed in the Euclidean case that the (linear) quantitative stability estimate depends only on whether the exponent p p is > 2 >2 or ≤ 2 \leq 2 . In the critical case, our dimensional constraint coincides with the seminal result of Figalli and Glaudo [ 28 ] but we notice a striking dependence on the exponent p p in the subcritical regime as well which is not present in the flat case. Our technique is an amalgamation of Figalli and Glaudo’s method and builds upon a series of new and novel estimates on the interaction of hyperbolic bubbles and their derivatives and improved eigenfunction integrability estimates. Since the conformal group coincides with the isometry group of the hyperbolic space, we perceive a remarkable distinction in arguments and techniques to achieve our main results compared to that of the Euclidean case.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 58J05, 46E35, 35A23, 35J61

1.
Introduction

In this article, we continue our study on the stability of Poincaré-Sobolev inequalities in the hyperbolic space. The focus of this article is on the multi-bubble case. Let us first introduce to the reader the Sobolev embedding, generally refer to as Poincaré-Sobolev inequality on the hyperbolic space. Throughout this article,
\mathbb{B}^{n}
denotes the Poincaré ball model of the hyperbolic space equipped with the metric
g:=\left(\frac{2}{1-|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}\;{\rm d}x^{2}
and
\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}},\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
denote the Laplace-Beltrami operator and the gradient operator respectively, and
{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
denotes the volume element.

1.1.
Poincaré-Sobolev inequality on the hyperbolic space.

Let
n\geq 3
and
\lambda\leq\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}
and
1<p\leq 2^{\star}-1.
Then there exists a best constant
S_{\lambda,p}:=S_{\lambda,p}(\mathbb{B}^{n})>0
such that

(1.1)

\displaystyle S_{\lambda,p}\left(\penalty\ \int\limits_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|u|^{p+1}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)^{\frac{2}{p+1}}\leq\int\limits_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|^{2}-\lambda u^{2}\right)\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

holds for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n}),
where
2^{\star}=\frac{2n}{n-2}.

By density (
1.1
) continues to hold for every
u
belonging to the the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
with respect to the norm

(1.2)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\lambda}=\left(\int\limits_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|^{2}-\lambda u^{2}\right)\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The quantity
\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}):=\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}
is the
L^{2}
-bottom of the spectrum of
-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
defined by

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})=\inf_{\|u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}=1}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}^{2}.

As a result, if
\lambda<\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4},
then the closure is the classical Sobolev space
H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}).
Otherwise, the closure is a larger space that we will denote by
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
and which contains non-square-integrable functions. We refer to Section
2
for a precise definition of
H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}).

The inequality (
1.1
) was first obtained by Mancini and Sandeep in
[
41
]
and in the same article, they also proved the existence of optimizers under appropriate assumptions on
n,\lambda
and
p.
In particular, they showed that under the assumption that

\displaystyle\mbox{{(\bf H1)}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \begin{cases}\lambda<\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4},\ \ \ \mbox{when}\ 1<p<\frac{n+2}{n-2},\ \mbox{and}\ n\geq 3,\\
\\
\frac{n(n-2)}{4}<\lambda<\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4},\ \ \ \mbox{when}\ p=\frac{n+2}{n-2},\ \mbox{and}\ n\geq 4,\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \end{cases}

there always exists a strictly positive, radially symmetric and decreasing extremizer
\mathcal{U}:=\mathcal{U}_{n,\lambda,p}
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}).
For simplicity of notations we will always denote the radially symmetric solution by
\mathcal{U}
and the dependence of
n,\lambda
and
p
will be implicitly assumed. It is straightforward to verify that subject to the normalization

(1.3)

\displaystyle\bullet\ \|\mathcal{U}\|_{\lambda}^{2}=S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}},\ \mbox{in addition to}\ \mathcal{U}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}),\mathcal{U}>0\ \mbox{radially symmetric and decreasing},

the obtained extremizer is a positive solution to the Euler-Lagrange equation

(1.4)

\displaystyle-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u-\lambda u=|u|^{p-1}u\penalty\ \ \ \ \ u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}).

The equation (
1.4
) as well as the inequality (
1.1
) is invariant under the conformal group of the ball model, which in this case coincides with the isometry group of the ball model and is generated by the hyperbolic translations
\tau_{b},b\in\mathbb{B}^{n}.
In analogy with the Euclidean case
\tau_{b}
can be thought of as the translation with takes
0
to
b.
In
[
41
]
Mancini and Sandeep also classified the positive solutions of (
1.4
) and which in turn provides the classification of the extremizers of (
1.1
). Their results are as follows: Under the assumptions
(H1)
the
n
-dimensional manifold defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{0}:=\{\mathcal{U}[b]:=\mathcal{U}\circ\tau_{b}:b\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\}

consists of all the positive solutions to (
1.4
) and
c\mathcal{Z}_{0},\ c\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
consists of all the nontrivial extremizers of (
1.1
). Henceforth we shall call the elements of
\mathcal{Z}_{0}
a
hyperbolic bubble
.

Thanks to (
1.1
) the solutions of (
1.4
) are the critical points of the energy functional

(1.5)

\displaystyle I_{\lambda}(u)=\frac{1}{2}\int\limits_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|^{2}-\lambda u^{2}\right)\penalty\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\frac{1}{p+1}\int\limits_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|u|^{p+1}\penalty\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

Using the conformal invariance of the norms stated in Section
2
, in particular, Lemma
2.1
, it is easy to see that all the hyperbolic bubbles have the same energy

I_{\lambda}(\mathcal{U}\circ\tau_{b})=\frac{p-1}{2(p+1)}S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}.

1.2.
The Euclidean Sobolev inequality.

The classical Sobolev inequality in
\mathbb{R}^{n},

n\geq 3,
asserts that there exists a best constant
S:=S(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that

(1.6)

\displaystyle S\left(\penalty\ \int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|u|^{2^{\star}}{\rm d}x\right)^{2/2^{\star}}\leq\int\limits_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla u|^{2}\ {\rm d}x

holds for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),
where
2^{\star}=\frac{2n}{n-2}.
By density argument, the inequality (
1.6
) continues to hold for all
u

satisfying
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty,
and
\mathcal{L}^{n}(\{|u|>t\})<\infty
for every
t>0,
where
\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
denotes the
L^{2}
-norm and
\mathcal{L}^{n}
denotes the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Henceforth we shall denote the closure by
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

The value of
S
is known and the equality cases in (
1.6
) have been well studied. The inequality (
1.6
) is invariant under the action of the conformal group of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
composed of the translations, dilations and inversions: for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\mu>0,
and
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
define
T_{z,\mu}(u)=\mu^{\frac{n-2}{2}}u(\mu(\cdot-z)),
then both the norms in (
1.6
) are preserved. It is well known that (
1.6
) is achieved if and only if
u
is a constant multiple of the
Aubin-Talenti
bubbles
[
1
,
53
]

(1.7)

\displaystyle U[z,\mu](x)=(n(n-2))^{\frac{n-2}{4}}\mu^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\frac{1}{(1+\mu^{2}|x-z|^{2})^{\frac{n-2}{2}}},\quad z\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\,\mu>0.

Therefore the set of equality cases in (
1.6
) forms a
(n+2)
dimensional manifold. The choice of the dimensional constant in the definition of
U[z,\mu]=T_{z,\mu}(U[0,1])
ensures that
U
is a positive solution of the corresponding Euler-Lagrange equation

(1.8)

\displaystyle-\Delta u=|u|^{2^{\star}-2}u\ \mbox{ in }\ \mathbb{R}^{n}.

Thanks to the Sobolev inequality, all solutions to (
1.8
) are the critical points of

\displaystyle J(v)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|\nabla v|^{2}\ dx-\frac{1}{2^{\star}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|v|^{2^{\star}}\ dx,\ \ \ \ v\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

and moreover all the Aubin-Talenti bubbles have the same energy
J(U[z,\lambda])=\frac{1}{n}S(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\frac{n}{2}}.

1.3.
The quantitative stability problem

In connection with Aubin and Talenti’s results on the equality cases of the Euclidean Sobolev inequality, Brezis and Lieb
[
12
]
asked the following: if
u
almost optimizes (
1.6
) i.e. if the deficit

\displaystyle\delta_{Eucl}(u)=\|\nabla u\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}-S\|u\|^{2}_{L^{2^{\star}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

is small then is it true that
u
is close, in some appropriate sense, to the family of Aubin-Talenti bubbles?
This question is positively answered by Bianchi and Egnell
[
7
]
nearly thirty years ago in the following quantitative form

\displaystyle\inf_{\mu\in\mathbb{R},z\in\mathbb{R}^{n},c\in\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{\|\nabla u-cU[z,\mu]\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}}{\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}}\right)\leq C(n)\delta_{Eucl}(u)^{\frac{1}{2}},

strengthening the original results of Brezis and Lieb in bounded domains.
Here it is important to remark that the norm
\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}
is the strongest possible norm and the exponent
\frac{1}{2}
is sharp, in the sense that if we replace
\frac{1}{2}
by any other exponent strictly bigger than
\frac{1}{2}
then the inequality fails as
\delta_{Eucl}(u)\to 0.

The stability problem has been a centre of attraction for several authors for quite some time. Many possible generalizations and improvements in several directions have appeared in the literature. A notable generalization in this direction is the stability for
p
-Sobolev inequality for
p\neq 2.
In
[
25
]
the authors
obtained results similar to Brezis and Lieb but the Bianchi-Egnell type result was open for a long time. After some remarkable results of Cianchi
[
14
]
, Cianchi-Fusco-Maggi-Pratelli
[
15
]
, Figalli-Neumayer
[
29
]
finally Figalli and Zhang
[
31
]
obtained sharp quantitative stability results for
p\neq 2
. Our goal for this article is somewhat different what we are going to describe now.

Another remarkable direction is the stability problem from the Euler-Lagrange point of view initiated by Cirallo-Figalli and Maggi
[
17
]
. The stability question can also be posed as follows: if
u
almost solves the corresponding Euler-Lagrange equation then is it true that
u
is close to the family of Aubin-Talenti bubbles in a quantitative way? The problem is far more challenging and generally false unless some more hypotheses are imposed upon it. To see this note that
u=U[-Re_{1},1]+U[Re_{1},1]
almost solves (
1.8
) i.e.
\|\Delta u+|u|^{p-1}u\|_{H^{-1}}
is small but clearly
u
is not close to the family of Aubin-Talenti bubbles. Here it is important to remark that the interaction between the bubbles

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla U[-Re_{1},1]\cdot\nabla U[Re_{1},1]\ dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U[-Re_{1},1]^{p}U[Re_{1},1]\ dx\approx R^{2-n}=o(1)\ \ \mbox{as}\ \ R\rightarrow\infty,

which makes
\|\Delta u+|u|^{p-1}u\|_{H^{-1}}
small.

This shortcoming can easily be addressed by rephrasing the question to ‘Does
u
close to a sum of Aubin-Talenti bubbles?’ Yet this is not true as there are examples of non-trivial sign-changing solutions which are not the sum of Aubin-Talenti bubbles
[
20
]
. However, the classification results of Gidas, Ni and Nirenberg ensure all the positive solutions to (
1.8
)
are the Aubin-Talenti bubbles
[
37
]
.

A seminal work of Struwe
[
52
]
(also see
[
51
]
) which dates back to the results of Bianchi and Egnell asserts that imposing a non-negativity assumption on
u
one can get a non-quantitative stability result.

Theorem A

(Struwe, 1984)
.

Let
n\geq 3
and
N\geq 1
be positive integers. Let
\{u_{m}\}\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a sequence of nonnegative functions such that
(N-\frac{1}{2})S^{n}\leq\|\nabla u_{m}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq(N-\frac{1}{2})S^{n},
and assume that

\displaystyle\|\Delta u_{m}+u_{m}^{2^{\star}-1}\|_{H^{-1}}\rightarrow 0\ \ \mbox{as}\ \ m\rightarrow\infty.

Then there exists a sequence
(z_{1}^{m},\ldots,z_{N}^{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
of
N
-tuple of points in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and sequence
(\lambda_{1}^{m},\ldots,\lambda_{N}^{m})
of
N
-tuple of positive real numbers such that

\displaystyle\Big\|\nabla\left(u_{m}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}U[z_{i}^{m},\lambda_{i}^{m}]\right)\Big\|_{L^{2}}\rightarrow 0\ \ \mbox{as}\ \ m\rightarrow\infty.

Moreover the Aubin-Talenti bubbles
U[z_{i}^{m},\lambda_{i}^{m}]
,
U[z_{j}^{m},\lambda_{j}^{m}]
, for
i\neq j
do not interact with each other at the
H^{1}
-level

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\nabla U[z_{i}^{m},\lambda_{i}^{m}]\cdot\nabla U[z_{j}^{m},\lambda_{j}^{m}]\ dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U[z_{i}^{m},\lambda_{i}^{m}]^{2^{\star}-1}U[z_{j}^{m},\lambda_{j}^{m}]\ dx

\displaystyle\approx\min\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}^{m}}{\lambda_{j}^{m}},\frac{\lambda_{j}^{m}}{\lambda_{i}^{m}},\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{m}\lambda_{j}^{m}|z_{i}^{m}-z_{j}^{m}|^{2}}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\rightarrow 0\ \ \mbox{as}\ \ m\rightarrow\infty.

A sharp quantitative form of Struwe decomposition has recently been obtained by Figalli and Glaudo
[
28
]
. A clever way of getting around the problem of sign-changing
u
is by phrasing the problem locally around the sum of Aubin-Talenti bubbles as nicely done by Cirallo-Figalli-Maggi
[
17
]
for single bubble case and Figalli-Galudo
[
28
]
for the multi-bubble case in dim
3\leq n\leq 5
, and more recently by Deng-Sun and Wei
[
19
]
in dimension
n\geq 6.
It is remarkable that for multi-bubble cases the linear dependence of
\|\Delta u+|u|^{p-1}u\|
heavily relies on the underlying dimension. In other words, unlike the single bubble case, the form of quantitative stability results for the multi-bubble case, the underlying dimension plays a significant role and it seems this difference results from the behaviour of the interacting bubbles
[
28
,
19
]
.
To state their results we recall the definition of weak interaction among the bubbles.

A family of Aubin-Talenti bubbles
\{U[z_{i},\lambda_{i}]\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
is said to be
\delta
-interacting if the interaction between any two bubbles is less than
\delta
i.e.

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\neq j\leq N}\min\left(\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{j}},\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{i}},\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}|z_{i}-z_{j}|^{2}}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\leq\delta.

Note that the definition makes sense only if there is more than one bubble. The results obtained so far in the Euclidean case have been summarized in the following theorem.

Theorem B
.

Let
n\geq 3
and
N\geq 1
be positive integers. There exist a small constant
\delta=\delta(n,N)
and a large constant
C=C(n,N)>0
such that the following statement holds: let
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a function such that

\displaystyle\Big\|\nabla u-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla\tilde{U}\Big\|_{L^{2}}\leq\delta

where
\{\tilde{U}_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
is a
\delta
-interaction family of
1
1

1

when
N=1
the underlined statement should be omitted.
Aubin-Talenti bubbles.
Then there exists
N
Aubin-Talenti bubbles
U_{1},\ldots,U_{N}
such that

(a)

Cirallo-Figalli-Maggi
[
17
]
. If
N=1
then

\displaystyle\Big\|\nabla u-\nabla U_{1}\Big\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\Delta u+|u|^{2^{\star}-2}u\|_{H^{-1}}.

(b)

Figalli-Glaudo
[
28
]
. If
N>1
and
3\leq n\leq 5
then

(1.9)

\displaystyle\Big\|\nabla u-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla U_{i}\Big\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\|\Delta u+|u|^{2^{\star}-2}u\|_{H^{-1}}.

Moreover, for any
i\neq k
, the interaction between the bubbles can be estimated
as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U_{i}^{2^{\star}-1}U_{k}\ dx\leq C\|\Delta u+|u|^{2^{\star}-2}u\|_{H^{-1}}.

Furthermore, the dimensional restriction is optimal in the sense that for
n\geq 6
and
N=2
there exists a sequence
\{u_{R}\}\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that (
1.9
) fails to hold for any
C=C(n)
as
R\rightarrow\infty.

(c)

Ding-Sun-Wei
[
19
]
. For
n\geq 6
the following optimal
2
2

2

optimality in the same sense as in (b).
inequality holds

\displaystyle\Big\|\nabla u-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\nabla U_{i}\Big\|_{L^{2}}\leq C\begin{cases}\|\Delta u+|u|^{2^{\star}-2}u\|_{H^{-1}}\big|\ln\|\Delta u+|u|^{2^{\star}-2}u\|_{H^{-1}}\big|^{\frac{1}{2}},\ \ \mbox{if}\ \ n=6,\\
\\
\|\Delta u+|u|^{2^{\star}-2}u\|_{H^{-1}}^{\frac{n+2}{2(n-2)}},\ \ \mbox{if}\ \ n\geq 7.\end{cases}

Before proceeding to the next section let us mention to the interested readers some existing stability results in the literature. We refer to the beautiful treatises
[
34
,
27
,
40
,
16
,
14
,

,
13
,
22
,
21

,
10
,
23
,
44
,
47
,
31
,
55
,
2
,
43
,
49
]
which covers, for example, the stability of isoperimetric inequality,
1
-Sobolev inequality, Caffarelli-Kohn-Nirenberg inequality, log-Sobolev inequality, Gagliardo-Nirenberg-Sobolev inequality, Hardy-Littlewood-Sobolev,
logarithmic Hardy-Littlewood-Sobolev inequality on the Euclidean space. On the values of optimal constant
C
in the Bianchi-Egnell result we refer to
[
24
]
(see also
[
38
]
). On the stability of isoperimetric inequality on the sphere see
[
8
]
and on the hyperbolic space, we refer to
[
9
]
.
See also the recent articles
[
32
]
for stability results on
\mathbb{S}^{1}(1/\sqrt{n-2})\times S^{1}(1)
and
[
26
,
45
]
for related stability on a general Riemannian manifold.

For the critical point of view, other than the results
[
17
,
28
,
19
]
mentioned above, one can consult the beautiful monographs
[
18
,
39
]
for the Euclidean isoperimetric inequality. The beautiful survey article by Fusco

[
33
]
gives a broad description of the stability results concerning several other related geometric and functional inequalities.

1.4.
Known results in the hyperbolic space

In a recent article
[
5
]
, we studied the quantitative stability of the Poincaré-Sobolev inequality in the spirit of Bianchi and Egnell
[
7
]
. The bottom of the spectrum of
-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
being positive, the hyperbolic space admits sub-critical Sobolev inequality as well (i.e.
p<2^{\star}-1
) and we established the stability for both critical and subcritical Sobolev inequalities.

The non-quantitative form of the Poincaré Sobolev inequality on the hyperbolic space have been obtained nearly a decade ago by the first author jointly with Sandeep
[
6
]
. They establish the following Struwe-type profile decomposition both for the subcritical and the critical case.
Needless to say in the subcritical case there are no Aubin-Talenti bubbles and hence there are only hyperbolic bubbles present in the profile decomposition. However, in the critical case one can not exclude the possibility of the presence of suitably localized Aubin-Talenti bubbles as indicated below.

For the critical case
p=2^{\star}-1,
there are two types of bubbles. The hyperbolic bubbles which are of the form
\mathfrak{u}_{k}=\mathcal{U}\circ\tau_{b_{k}}
for some sequence
b_{k}\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
and localized Aubin-Talenti bubbles which are of the form

\displaystyle\mathfrak{v}_{k}=\left(\frac{1-|x|^{2}}{2}\right)^{\frac{n-2}{2}}\phi(x)U[x_{0},\epsilon_{k}]

where
\epsilon_{k}\rightarrow 0+
and
\phi
is a smooth cut-off function such that
0\leq\phi\leq 1,
and
\phi\equiv 1
in a neighbourhood of
x_{0},
and
U[x_{0},\epsilon_{k}]
are the standard Aubin-Talenti bubbles defined above. Using the compact
L^{2}_{\tiny{loc}}
-embedding, it is easy to verify that
I_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{v}_{k})=J(U[x_{0},\epsilon_{k}])+o(1)
as
\epsilon_{k}\rightarrow 0.

As a result the hyperbolic bubbles have energy
I_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{u}_{k}^{j})=\frac{1}{n}S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{n}{2}}
for all
j
while the Aubin-Talenti bubble has energy
I_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{v}_{k}^{j})=\frac{1}{n}S^{\frac{n}{2}}+o(1)
as
\epsilon_{k}\rightarrow 0+
for all
j.

Theorem C

(Bhakta and Sandeep
[
6
]
)
.

Let
n\geq 3,\lambda,p
satisfies the hypothesis
(H1)
. Let
\{u_{m}\}\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
be a sequence such that
I_{\lambda}(u_{m})\rightarrow d
and

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u_{m}+\lambda u_{m}+|u_{m}|^{p-1}u_{m}\|_{H^{-1}}\rightarrow 0,\ \ \mbox{as}\ \ m\rightarrow\infty.

Then there exists
N_{1},N_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
and functions
\mathfrak{u}_{m}^{j}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}),1\leq j\leq N_{1},\mathfrak{v}_{m}^{j},1\leq j\leq N_{2}
and
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
such that up to a subsequence

\displaystyle u_{m}=u+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{1}}\mathfrak{u}_{m}^{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{2}}\mathfrak{v}_{m}^{j}+o(1)\ \ in\ H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}),

where
I_{\lambda}^{\prime}(u)=0
in
H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
\mathfrak{u}_{m}^{j},\mathfrak{v}_{m}^{j}
are defined as above. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle d=\begin{cases}I_{\lambda}(u)+N_{1}\frac{p-1}{2(p+1)}S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ p<2^{\star}-1,\\
\\
I_{\lambda}(u)+\frac{N_{1}}{n}S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{n}{2}}+\frac{N_{2}}{n}S^{\frac{n}{2}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ p=2^{\star}-1.\end{cases}

Remark 1.1
.

If one or more kinds of bubbles are absent we will write
N_{i}=0,i=1,2.
For example if the Aubin-Talenti bubbles are absent from the profile decomposition we simply write
N_{2}=0.
Note that this is indeed the case if
p<2^{\star}-1
or
d<\frac{1}{n}S^{\frac{n}{2}}
for
p=2^{\star}-1.

Remark 1.2
.

It is worth noting that in the above theorem, we have imposed no sign assumption on the sequence
u_{m}
and hence the
u
, which is obtained by just taking the weak limit of
u_{m}
, may be different than the standard hyperbolic bubble. However, if we impose
u_{m}
are non-negative, then from the uniqueness of the positive solutions to (
1.4
) we obtain either
u\equiv 0
or
u=\mathcal{U}\circ\tau_{b}
for some
b\in\mathbb{B}^{n}.
In either case we have the energy quantization

\displaystyle d=\frac{\tilde{N}_{1}}{n}S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{n}{2}}+\frac{N_{2}}{n}S^{\frac{n}{2}},\ \ \mbox{if}\ \ p=2^{\star}-1,\ \ \mbox{or}\ \ d=\tilde{N}_{1}\frac{p-1}{2(p+1)}S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}},\ \ \mbox{if}\ \ p<2^{\star}-1,

where
\tilde{N}_{1}
equal to either
N_{1}
or
N_{1}+1.

Remark 1.3
.

In Section
3
we show that if
p=2^{\star}-1
and
d=\frac{N}{n}S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{n}{2}}
for some
N\in\mathbb{N}
then there exists an at most countable set
\Lambda
such that if
\lambda\not\in\Lambda
and
u_{m}\geq 0
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
C
then
N_{2}=0,
i.e. the Aubin-Talenti bubbles are absent in the profile decomposition. This is the starting point of our result and quite remarkably the proof relies on a very simple observation of the strict decreasing property of the function
\lambda\mapsto S_{\lambda,2^{\star}-1.}

1.5.
Main results of the article

In Section
4
we will show that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z_{i}],\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z_{k}]\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda\mathcal{U}[z_{i}]\mathcal{U}[z_{k}]\right)\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z_{i}]^{p}\mathcal{U}[z_{k}]\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\approx e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z_{i},z_{k})},

where

(1.10)

c(n,\lambda)=\frac{n-1+\sqrt{(n-1)^{2}-4\lambda}}{2},

is the rate of decay of the hyperbolic bubbles:
\mathcal{U}(x)\approx e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,0)}.

This estimate led us to the following definition.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{1}=\mathcal{U}[z_{1}],\ldots,\mathcal{U}_{N}=\mathcal{U}[z_{N}]
be a family of hyperbolic bubbles. We say that the family is
\delta
-interacting for some
\delta>0
if

(1.11)

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\neq k\leq N}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z_{i},z_{k})}\leq\delta.

If we consider for some
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
the family
\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq N,
then
the family is said to be
\delta
-interacting if in addition to (
1.11
)

\displaystyle\max_{1\leq i\leq N}\ |\alpha_{i}-1|\leq\delta

holds.

Therefore
\mathcal{U}_{i},\mathcal{U}_{k}
belongs to a
\delta
-interacting family if their
H^{1}
-scalar product is bounded by a constant multiple of

\delta.
Throughout the article, we shall use the following notations:

(1.12)

\displaystyle Q_{ik}=e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z_{i},z_{k})},\ \ Q_{k}=\max_{i\neq k}\ Q_{ik}\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ Q=\max_{k}\ Q_{k}

where
1\leq i,\,k\leq N.

The main result of this article is the following

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
3\leq n\leq 5
and
p>2
such that
p
and
\lambda
satisfy
(H1)
. For any
N\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a small constant
\delta=\delta(n,\lambda,p,N)>0
and a large constant
C=C(n,p,\lambda,N)>0
such that the following statement holds: let
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
be a function such that

\displaystyle\Big\|u-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}\Big\|_{\lambda}\leq\delta,

where
(\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}
is a
\delta
-interacting family of hyperbolic bubbles. Then there exists
N
hyperbolic bubbles
(\mathcal{U}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}
such that

\displaystyle\Big\|u-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{U}_{i}\Big\|_{\lambda}\leq C\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+|u|^{p-1}u\|_{H^{-1}}.

Moreover, for any
i\neq k,
the interaction between the bubbles can be estimated as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq C\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+|u|^{p-1}u\|_{H^{-1}}.

As an application of Theorem
1.1
and the profile decomposition Theorem
3.1
proved in Section
3
we have the following corollary.

Corollary 1.1
.

Let
3\leq n\leq 5
and
p>2
such that
p
and
\lambda
satisfy
(H1)
and
N\in\mathbb{N}.
Then there exists a small constant
\delta=\delta(n,\lambda,p,N)>0
and a large constant
C=C(n,p,\lambda,N)>0
and a countable subset
\Lambda
of
\mathbb{R}
if
p=2^{\star}-1
such that for
\lambda\not\in\Lambda
the following statement holds: For any
non-negative function
u\in H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
satisfying

\displaystyle\left(N-\delta\right)S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}\leq\|u\|^{2}_{\lambda}\leq\left(N-\delta\right)S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}},

there exists
N
hyperbolic bubbles
(\mathcal{U}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}
such that

\displaystyle\Big\|u-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{U}_{i}\Big\|_{\lambda}\leq C\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+u^{p}\|_{H^{-1}}.

Moreover, for any
i\neq k,
the interaction between the bubbles can be estimated as

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq C\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+|u|^{p}\|_{H^{-1}}.

After proving the stability result we look for the counter-example when
n\geq 3
and
1<p\leq 2.
We prove that there exists a one-parameter family
u_{R}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}),R\in(0,1)
such that the following estimate holds:

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u_{R}+\lambda u_{R}+|u_{R}|^{p-1}u_{R}\|_{L^{(p+1)^{\prime}}}\lesssim\begin{cases}\mathcal{D}|\log\mathcal{D}|^{-\frac{1}{2}},\quad\mbox{if}\ p=2\\
\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\mathcal{D}^{1+\alpha_{0}},\qquad\quad\ \mbox{if}\ 1<p<2.\end{cases}

where
\mathcal{D}
is the distance of
u_{R}
from the manifold of sum of hyperbolic bubbles and
\alpha_{0}>0
is a constant depending on
n,\lambda
and
p.
When we let
R\uparrow 1,
then
\mathcal{D}\rightarrow 0.
This proves that the stability estimate of Theorem
1.1
fails whenever
1<p\leq 2,
in particular, in dimension
n\geq 6.
Regarding Struwe decomposition in the hyperbolic space, we prove that the positive part of
u_{R},u_{R}^{+}:=\max\{u_{R},0\}
also satisfies similar estimates and hence strengthening the counter-example for non-negative functions. The main result concerning the counterexample is stated in Section
9
, Theorem
9.1
and proved in subsequent sections.

Remark 1.4
.

We remark that the case
N=1
has already been studied in
[
5
]
and the (linear stability) result holds in any dimension
n\geq 3
and
p\in(1,2^{\star}-1].
For
N\geq 2
,
note that in the statement of Corollary
1.1
for the critical case
p=2^{\star}-1
we claim to prove the absence of Aubin-Talenti bubbles which significantly improves the profile decomposition result of
[
6
]
stated in Theorem
C
. However, we have to pay the price by excluding a countable set which is still, in our humble opinion, a nice result.
Our study reveals that the stability of the hyperbolic space in low dimension
3\leq n\leq 5
bear resembles that of the Euclidean space even though the kernel of the linearized operator in the hyperbolic space is
n
-dimensional. The stability result of
[
19
]
in dimension
n\geq 6
for the hyperbolic space is still unknown. However, we expect similar results should hold in the hyperbolic space as well which we plan to study in a forthcoming article.

1.6.
Main hurdles, Novelty and Strategy of the proof

We shall now discuss the main difficulties that we encounter while proving Theorem
1.1
and we briefly describe the new tools and the strategy of the proof.

•

As discussed above, while considering the multi-bubble scenario in the
critical case
we cannot ignore the existence of Aubin-Talenti bubbles. Hence we must take into account the interaction between the hyperbolic bubbles and the Aubin-Talenti bubbles, which is a very challenging task. In this article as a first step, we look for the situation where the Aubin-Talenti bubbles are absent. To our surprise, and even more surprising the simplicity of the proof, we found that at energy level integer multiple of
\frac{1}{n}S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{n}{2}}
,
p=2^{\star}-1
and outside a countable set
\Lambda
of
\lambda
’s there exist only hyperbolic bubbles. The set is characterized by

(1.13)

\displaystyle\Lambda=\left\{\lambda\in\left[\frac{n(n-2)}{4},\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}\right]\ :\ \left(\frac{S_{\lambda,p}}{S}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}\right\}.

We prove in Section
3
that
\Lambda
is at most countable. As a result, we could make the situation slightly simpler by considering
\lambda
in the complement of
\Lambda.
Then we have to only consider the interaction between the
hyperbolic bubbles
. However, working with hyperbolic bubbles poses many non-trivial and new difficulties which we try to explain briefly.

•

To our knowledge, the interaction integrals of hyperbolic bubbles are not known. As a first step, we obtained all the necessary and sharp interaction estimates among the bubbles. We obtain a series of new and novel estimates on the interactions of bubbles and their derivatives. We want to stress that these estimates are quite geometric in nature and very explicit, i.e., it depends largely on the geometry of the hyperbolic space. Another novelty of this article is that we used explicit formulas of the hyperbolic distance and we were able to reduce the computations to a manageable integral evaluation. We found that all the interaction estimates are exponentially decaying in the hyperbolic distance between the points where the bubbles are most concentrated. More precisely, we prove that if
\mathcal{U}[z_{1}],\mathcal{U}[z_{2}]
are two bubbles then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z_{1}]^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}[z_{2}]^{\beta}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\approx\begin{cases}e^{-c(n,\lambda)\min\{\alpha,\beta\}d(z_{1},z_{2})}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ \alpha\neq\beta,\\
d(z_{1},z_{2})e^{-c(n,\lambda)\min\{\alpha,\beta\}d(z_{1},z_{2})}\ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ \alpha=\beta,\end{cases}

whenever,
\alpha+\beta\geq 2,
and where
d(z_{1},z_{2})
is the hyperbolic distance between
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
and the constant in
\approx
depends on
|\alpha-\beta|
and other natural parameters.

•

A significant challenge comes while estimating the interaction between a bubble
\mathcal{U}[z_{1}]
and the spacial derivative
V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[z_{2}])
of another bubble
\mathcal{U}[z_{2}]
(see Appendix for the definition of
V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[z_{2}])
) where
j
-denoted the direction (say)
e_{j}.
Without loss of generality we may assume
z_{1}=z
and
z_{2}=0.
It turns out that the interaction

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[0])\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

is very sensitive to the position of the point
z.
Some simple computations lead to the fact that if
z
lies on the hyperplane
\{x|\ x\cdot e_{j}=0\}
then the interaction vanishes. More generally we can prove: if
z
lies in the negative half
\{x|\ x\cdot e_{j}<0\}
then the interaction is
\approx_{z}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z,0)}
and if
z
lies in the positive half
\{x|\ x\cdot e_{j}>0\}
then the interaction is
\approx_{z}-e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z,0)}.
This is not a good situation to be in because constant
C
in Theorem
1.1
should depend only on the weak interaction strength
\delta
, not on the location of the bubbles on which we do not have any information whatsoever.
However, we manage to prove that if
z
lies
\kappa>0
distance away from the hyperplane i.e.
z\in\{x|\ |x\cdot e_{j}|\geq\kappa\}
then the interaction is
\approx_{\kappa}\pm\ e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z,0)},
where the dependence of the constant
\approx_{\kappa}
on
n,p,\lambda
is implicitly assumed. As a result, the interaction of two bubbles and the interaction of a bubble and the derivative of another bubble has the same decay estimates up to a sign.

•

The hyperbolic space does not admit any substitute of the conformal group of dilations as in the case of Euclidean geometry. This has both advantages and disadvantages. The advantage is that we have only bubble clusters and not bubble towers in the language of
[
19
]
. As a result, the computation of the interaction in the hyperbolic space turns out to be simpler than that of the Euclidean case. There is also a caveat while proving Theorem
1.1
as indicated in the next few bullet points.

•

The proof of the Theorem
1.1
follows the same strategy as in
[
28
]
. Given
u
we find a
\delta
-interacting family of hyperbolic bubbles
(\alpha_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}
by the minimization process

\inf_{\tilde{\alpha}_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\tilde{z}_{i}\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|u-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\alpha}_{i}\mathcal{U}[\tilde{z}_{i}]\|_{\lambda}
and we put
\rho=u-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}=u-\sigma.
We test
\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+|u|^{p-1}u
against
\rho
and performing some similar computations as in
[
28
, Theorem 3.3]
the proof boils down to the justification of the following two inequalities:

(i)

Improved spectral inequality.

Provided the weakly interaction
\delta
is sufficiently small, it holds that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\sigma^{p-1}\rho^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\ \frac{\tilde{c}}{p}\|\rho\|^{2}_{\lambda},\ \ \mbox{where}\quad\tilde{c}=\tilde{c}(n,N,\lambda,p)<1.

(ii)

Interaction integral estimates.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, if
\delta
is sufficiently small then

\displaystyle\max_{i\neq k}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim\|(\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\lambda)u+|u|^{p-1}u\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}.

•

The proof of (i) uses a localization argument as in
[
28
]
. The main difference is that the
L^{n}
-norm of the gradient is conformal and hence one can not expect the
L^{n}
-norm of the gradient of the localized function to be small. The substitute in the hyperbolic space is the
L^{\infty}
-norm of the gradient of the localized function. Thanks to the Poincaré inequality this poses no threat in our case.

•

The proof of
(ii)
in the Euclidean case relies heavily upon the interaction among the bubbles and the
\ mu
derivatives of another bubble. Since
u=\sigma+\rho
one can express
\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+|u|^{p-1}u
in the following manner

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho+\lambda\rho+p\sigma^{p-1}\rho+I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}=\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+|u|^{p-1}u,

where
I_{1}=\sigma^{p}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}
contains all the interactions between the bubbles.
In the Euclidean case one test the above equation against
\partial_{\mu}U_{k}
and derive that

\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}I_{1}\partial_{\mu}U_{k}\ dx\big|\approx\max_{i\neq k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U_{i}^{p}U_{k}\ dx.

The main reason for testing by
\partial_{\mu}U_{k}
over testing by a spacial derivative (i.e.
\partial_{x_{j}}U_{k}
) is that spatial derivative leads to much weaker interaction estimates
<<\max_{i\neq k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U_{i}^{p}U_{k}
in case the heights of the Aubin-Talenti bubbles are comparable i.e. if
U_{i}=U[z_{i},\mu_{i}],1\leq i\leq N,
then
\mu_{i}\approx\mu_{j}
(see A. Bahri
[
3
, Estimate (F11)]
). Note that the latter situation is precisely the case of the hyperbolic bubbles and the only possibility is to test by
V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})
. Thankfully our spacial derivative interaction almost behaves like the interaction of bubbles, but there is one caveat regarding the sign. There might be cancellation and
\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}I_{i}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})
might bring
very weak interaction.

•

We overcome this issue with the help of a geometric lemma
8.2
. The lemma suggests that if the family
(\mathcal{U}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}
sufficiently small and weakly interacting, then there exists a direction
e_{j}
such that up to hyperbolic translations, orthogonal transformations and rearrangement of indices
z_{N}=0
and all other
z_{i}
lies in the negative half of the plane
\{x|\ x\cdot e_{j}<0\}.
Moreover, all
z_{i},i\neq N
lie
at a positive distance away from the plane
\{x|\ x\cdot e_{j}=0\}
. The proof of the lemma is purely geometric and makes the most use of the properties of the reflection in plane and inversion in spheres.

•

In the next step we look for the counter-example of linear stability for
1<p\leq 2.
We proceed as in
[
28
]
. Let us denote
U=\mathcal{U}[-Re_{1}],V=\mathcal{U}[Re_{1}],
where
R\in(0,1)
and put
u_{R}=U+V+\rho.

Then we can estimate

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u_{R}+\lambda u_{R}+|u_{R}|^{p-1}u_{R}=\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho+\lambda\rho+p(U+V)^{p-1}\rho+f+O(|\rho|^{p})

where
f=(U+V)^{p}-U^{p}-V^{p}.
Of course, solving
\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho+\lambda\rho+p(U+V)^{p-1}\rho+f=0
and
satisfying the orthogonality condition is not possible and therefore we have to look for a perturbed problem

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho+\lambda\rho+p(U+V)^{p-1}\rho+\tilde{f}=0,

where
\tilde{f}
is such that
f-\tilde{f}
belongs to the finite-dimensional space spanned by the eigenvectors
of the operator
(-\Delta-\lambda)/(U+V)^{p-1}.
As a result by Poincaré-Sobolev inequality we get

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u_{R}+\lambda u_{R}+|u_{R}|^{p-1}u_{R}\|_{L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}}\lesssim\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{p},

where
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}
is an equivalent
H^{1}
-norm defined in Section
2
.
Moreover we can estimate
\|\rho\|_{\lambda}\approx\|\tilde{f}\|_{H^{-1}}
and
\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}}\lesssim\sum_{\Psi}|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\Psi{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|,
where
\Psi
solves

(1.14)

\displaystyle-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi-\lambda\Psi=\mu(U+V)^{p-1}\Psi.

In
[
28
]
, the authors showed that
\|f\|_{L^{2}}=o(\|f\|_{H^{-1}})
and hence one obtain
\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u_{R}+\lambda u_{R}+|u_{R}|^{p-1}u_{R}\|_{L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}}=o(\|\rho\|_{\lambda}).
Then they proceed to show that
\|\rho\|_{\lambda}
approximates the distance of
u_{R}
from the manifold of the sum of two Aubin-Talenti bubbles and concluding the construction of counter-example in dimension
n\geq 6.

•

In the hyperbolic space, thanks to Poincaré inequality
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}\lesssim\|f\|_{L^{2}}
and hence we need a major diversion from the line of reasoning from that of the Euclidean case. We achieve this in two steps:

(a)

A delicate eigenfunction estimates of (
1.14
). We can show that for sufficiently small
\alpha_{0}
the uniform bound

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi^{2}}{(U+V)^{\alpha_{0}}}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim_{\mu,\alpha_{0}}1,

holds.

(b)

And an improvement of the integral interaction estimates in half ball. Denote
\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}:=\{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\ |\ x_{1}>0\}
then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}}U^{\alpha}V^{\beta(p-1)}\approx e^{-c(n,\lambda)\frac{\alpha+\beta(p-1)}{2}d(-Re_{1},Re_{1})},\ \ \mbox{if}\ \alpha>\beta(p-1).

With the help of these two results and a well-derived lower bound on
\|f\|_{H^{-1}}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}}f\Psi\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right|=o(\|f\|_{H^{-1}}).

Once these estimates are achieved one can follow the ideas of Figalli and Glaudo to complete the proof. However, we emphasize that several technical difficulties arise in the hyperbolic space which need to be addressed separately. We derived these technical results either in the appendix or some of them whenever they arise.

The organization of the article is as follows:

Section 1:

Introduction section, contains a brief background on the stability problem in the Euclidean space, known results in the hyperbolic space, the main result of this article and a short subsection about major challenges and how we addressed it.

Section 2:

Contains the basics of the hyperbolic space.

Section 3:

This section is devoted to the improvement of the Struwe-type profile decomposition on the hyperbolic space. In particular, we obtained instances when Aubin-Talenti bubbles are absent in the profile decomposition.

Section 4:

This is one of the core sections of this article. In this section, we proved all the interaction estimates namely (a) interaction between two bubbles, (b) interaction among three bubbles, and (c) interaction between a bubble and the spacial derivation of another bubble. This section is slightly technical but all the results obtained, to the best of the knowledge of the authors, are all new.

Section 5:

Contains the proof of the main theorem, namely, Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
1.1

assuming (i) improved spectral inequality which is proved in Section 7 and (ii) Interaction integral estimates,
which is proved in Section 8.

Section 6:

Another slightly technical section containing the core of the localization argument.

Section 9:

Set up and technical results concerning the counter-example in the case
1<p\leq 2.
The main result is Theorem
9.1
.

Section 10:

Contains the proof of Theorem
9.1
.

Appendix:

We recalled some technical, some known and some probably unknown, results needed for the article.

1.7.
Notations

•

A point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
will be denoted by
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}).
For a point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

x_{j}
will denote the
j
-th component of
x.

•

n=
dimension,
N=
number of bubbles,
\delta=
interaction strength,
p+1=
exponent in Sobolev inequality and
\lambda=
spectral parameter.

•

The notation
T\lesssim S
(respectively
T\gtrsim S
) means
T\leq CS
(respectively
T\geq CS
) for some constant
C
that depends only on the given data

n,N,p,\lambda.
We denote
T\approx S
if both
T\lesssim S
and
T\gtrsim S
hold.

•

The notation
T\lesssim_{p,q,r}S
means
T\leq CS
for some constant
C
that depends on the parameters
p,q,r
as well as on
n,N,\lambda,p.

•

x\cdot y=\sum_{j=1}^{n}x_{j}y_{j}
denotes the standard dot product in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
|\cdot|
the associated norm.

•

\{e_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq n}
denotes the standard basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n},
i.e
l
-th component of
e_{j}
is
\delta_{jl}.

•

We denote by
B_{E}(a,r)
the Euclidean ball
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |\ |x-a|<r\}
and
S(a,r)
the Euclidean sphere
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |\ |x-a|=r\}.
The hyperbolic ball will be denoted by
B(a,r)
which is defined in the next section.

•

The notation
\|\cdot\|_{L^{r}}
will denote the
L^{r}
-norm with respect to the measure
{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

•

Given dimension
n\geq 3
,
S
denotes the best Sobolev constant in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and given
\lambda,p
as in (
1.1
),
S_{\lambda,\,p}
denotes the best Poincaré-Sobolev inequality in
\mathbb{B}^{n}.
In the later case if
p=2^{\star}-1
then we denote
S_{\lambda,p}
by
S_{\lambda}.

2.
Basics on the hyperbolic space

The Euclidean unit ball
\mathsf{B}^{n}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:|x|^{2}<1\}
equipped with the Riemannian metric

\displaystyle{\rm d}s^{2}=\left(\frac{2}{1-|x|^{2}}\right)^{2}\,{\rm d}x^{2}

constitute the ball model for the hyperbolic
n
-space, where
{\rm d}x
is the standard Euclidean metric and
|x|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}
is the standard Euclidean length. By definition, the hyperbolic
n
-space is a
n
-dimensional complete, non-compact Riemannian manifold having constant sectional curvature equal to
-1
and any two manifolds sharing the above properties are isometric
[
46
]
. In this article, all our computations will involve only the ball model and will be denoted by
\mathbb{B}^{n}
. We denote the inner product on the tangent space of
\mathbb{B}^{n}
by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
and
the volume element is given by
{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\left(\frac{2}{1-|x|^{2}}\right)^{n}{\rm d}x,
where
{\rm d}x
denotes the
Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Let
\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
and
\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
denote gradient vector field
and Laplace-Beltrami operator respectively. Therefore, in terms of local (global) coordinates
\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
and
\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
takes the form:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\left(\frac{1-|x|^{2}}{2}\right)^{2}\nabla,\quad\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\left(\frac{1-|x|^{2}}{2}\right)^{2}\Delta+(n-2)\left(\frac{1-|x|^{2}}{2}\right)x\cdot\nabla,

where
\nabla,\Delta
is the standard Euclidean gradient vector field and Laplace operator respectively, and ‘
\cdot
’ denotes the
standard inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Hyperbolic distance on
\mathbb{B}^{n}.
The hyperbolic distance between two points
x
and
y
in
\mathbb{B}^{n}
will be denoted by
d(x,y).
The hyperbolic distance between

x
and the origin can be computed explicitly

\displaystyle\rho:=\,d(x,0)=\int_{0}^{|x|}\frac{2}{1-s^{2}}\,{\rm d}s\,=\,\log\frac{1+|x|}{1-|x|},

and therefore
|x|=\tanh\frac{\rho}{2}.
Moreover, the hyperbolic distance between
x,y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
is given by

\displaystyle d(x,y)=\cosh^{-1}\left(1+\dfrac{2|x-y|^{2}}{(1-|x|^{2})(1-|y|^{2})}\right).

In this article, we will use several simplifications of the above formula which can be derived easily. For example, the following two formulas will be used:

\displaystyle\cosh\left(\frac{d(x,y)}{2}\right)=\frac{\sqrt{1-2x\cdot y+|x|^{2}|y|^{2}}}{\sqrt{(1-|x|^{2})(1-|y|^{2})}}=\frac{|y||x-y^{*}|}{\sqrt{(1-|x|^{2})(1-|y|^{2})}}.

where
y^{*}=y/|y|^{2}.
Alternatively, using the formula
\cosh^{-1}(s)=\ln(s+\sqrt{s^{2}-1}),

s>1
one can deduce

\displaystyle\frac{d(x,y)}{2}=\ln\left(\frac{|y||x-y^{*}|+|x-y|}{\sqrt{(1-|x|^{2})(1-|y|^{2})}}\right).

Let
A:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an orthogonal transformation i.e.,
Ax\cdot Ay=x\cdot y
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}.
Then it follows from the formula of hyperbolic distance that
d(Ax,Ay)=d(x,y)
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}.
In other words, the hyperbolic distance is preserved under the action of the orthogonal group of
\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Geodesic balls in
\mathbb{B}^{n}
of radius
r
centred at
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
will be denoted by

B(x,r):=\{y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}:d(x,y)<r\}.

Next, we introduce the concept of hyperbolic translation.

2.1.
Hyperbolic translation

We first recall some basic notions of reflections and inversions.

2.1.1.
Reflections about a plane

Let
a
be a non-zero vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t
be a real number. Consider the hyperplane in
\mathbb{R}^{n}

\displaystyle P(a,t)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |\ \frac{a}{|a|}\cdot x=t\}.

The reflection
\rho_{a,t}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
about the plane
P(a,t)
is defined by

\displaystyle\rho_{a,t}(x)=x+2\left(t-\frac{a}{|a|}\cdot x\right)\frac{a}{|a|}.

When
t=0
we will simply denote the reflection by
\rho_{a}.
It is easy to verify that
\rho_{a}
sends the positive half plane
\{x\ |\ a\cdot x>0\}
to the negative half
\{x\ |\ a\cdot x<0\}
and vice versa, and leaves the plane
P_{a,0}
invariant.

2.1.2.
Inversion about a sphere

Let
a
be a point in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and let
r
be a positive real number. The sphere of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of radius
r
centered at
a
is defined by

\displaystyle S(a,r)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |\ |x-a|=r\}.

The inversion
\sigma_{a,r}
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in the sphere
S(a,r)
is defined by the formula

\displaystyle\sigma_{a,r}(x)=a+\left(\frac{r}{|x-a|}\right)^{2}(x-a).

It is easy to verify that

\displaystyle|\sigma_{a,r}(x)-a||x-a|=r^{2},\ \ \ \ \mbox{for all}\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Hence
\sigma_{a,r}
sends sphere
S(a,r_{1})
to the sphere
S(a,\frac{r^{2}}{r_{1}})
for every
r_{1}>0.
When
r

is determined by
a
(for example
r^{2}=|a|^{2}-1
) we will simply use the notation
\sigma_{a}
instead of
\sigma_{a,r}.

2.1.3.
The hyperbolic translation

Given a point
a\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
|a|>1
and
r>0,
let
S(a,r):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |\ |x-a|=r\}
be the sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
with center
a
and radius
r
that intersects
S(0,1)
orthogonally. It is known that it is the case if and only if
r^{2}=|a|^{2}-1,
and hence
r
is determined by
a.
Let
\rho_{a}
denotes the reflection with respect to the plane
P_{a}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |\ x\cdot a=0\}
and
\sigma_{a}
denotes the inversion with respect to the sphere
S(a,r).
Then
\sigma_{a}\rho_{a}

leaves
\mathsf{B}^{n}
invariant (see
[
46
]
).

For
b\in\mathbb{B}^{n},
the hyperbolic translation
\tau_{b}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{B}^{n}
that takes
0
to
b
is defined by
\tau_{b}=\sigma_{b^{*}}\rho_{b^{*}}
and can be expressed by the following formula

(2.1)

\displaystyle\tau_{b}(x):=\frac{(1-|b|^{2})x+(|x|^{2}+2x\cdot b+1)b}{|b|^{2}|x|^{2}+2x\cdot b+1}

where
b^{*}=\frac{b}{|b|^{2}}.

It turns out that
\tau_{b}
is an isometry and forms the Möbius group of
\mathsf{B}^{n}
(see
[
46
]
, Theorem 4.4.6) for details and further discussions on isometries. Note that
\tau_{-b}=\sigma_{-b^{*}}\rho_{-b^{*}}
is the hyperbolic translation that takes
b
to the origin. In other words, the hyperbolic translation that takes
b
to the origin is the composition of the reflection
\rho_{-b^{*}}
and the inversion
\sigma_{-b^{*}}.

For the convenience of the reader, we list several well-known properties of the hyperbolic translation in the next lemma. The proof follows by now standard properties of the Möbius transformations on the ball model which we skip for brevity and refer to the book
[
50
]
.

Lemma 2.1
.

For
b\in\mathbb{B}^{n},
let
\tau_{b}
be the hyperbolic translation of
\mathbb{B}^{n}
by
b.
Then for every
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n}),
there holds,

(i)

\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(u\circ\tau_{b})=(\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u)\circ\tau_{b},\ \ \langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(u\circ\tau_{b}),\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(u\circ\tau_{b})\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\langle(\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u)\circ\tau_{b},(\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u)\circ\tau_{b}\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

(ii)

For every open subset
U
of
\mathbb{B}^{n}

\displaystyle\int_{U}|u\circ\tau_{b}|^{p}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\int_{\tau_{b}(U)}|u|^{p}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}},\ \mbox{for all}\ 1\leq p<\infty.

(iii)

For every
\phi,\psi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\phi(x)(\psi\circ\tau_{b})(x)\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\phi\circ\tau_{-b})(x)\psi(x)\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

By density, the above formulas hold as long as the integrals involved are finite.

2.2.
The Sobolev space
H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
:

Thanks to the Poincaré inequality, we define the Sobolev space on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
, denoted by
H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
, as the completion of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
with respect to the norm

\displaystyle\|u\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}:=\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|
is given by

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|:=\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u,\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

Using Poincaré inequality once again,
\|u\|_{\lambda}
defined by

\displaystyle\|u\|_{\lambda}^{2}:=\int\limits_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|^{2}-\lambda u^{2}\right){\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\penalty\ \hbox{ for all }u\in H(\mathbb{B}^{n}),

is an equivalent
H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
norm for
\lambda<\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}.
However, for
\lambda=\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4},

\|u\|_{\lambda}
is not an equivalent norm because, in general,
u
may fail to be square integrable. Moreover, thanks to Lemma
2.1
, the norms
\|u\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})},\|u\|_{\lambda}
are invariant under the action of isometries of the ball model. To distinguish between the norms we will call
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}
by
H^{1}_{\lambda}
-norm. Similarly, We will denote the

H^{1}_{\lambda}
-inner product by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\lambda}:=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{H^{1}}-\lambda\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}}
which generates the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}.

We also define the subset of
H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
consisting of radial functions

\displaystyle H^{1}_{\mbox{\tiny{rad}}}(\mathbb{B}^{n}):=\{u\ \mbox{radial}\ |\ \int_{0}^{\infty}\left[u^{2}(\rho)+(u^{\prime}(\rho))^{2}\right](\sinh\rho)^{n-1}\ d\rho<\infty\},

where
\prime
denotes the differentiation with respect
\rho.

We will also be needing the space
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
for
\Omega\subset\mathbb{B}^{n}
which is defined by the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}).

For a positive weight
w
we define the weighted
L^{2}
-space by

\displaystyle L^{2}_{w}(\mathbb{B}^{n}):=\Big\{u\ |\ \int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|u|^{2}w\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}<\infty\Big\}.

3.
On the absence of Aubin-Talenti bubbles

We investigate, in the critical case
p=2^{\star}-1
, under what conditions only the hyperbolic bubbles are present in Struwe-type profile decomposition. Because of the non-existence results of Sandeep and Mancini
[
41
]
in low dimensions and low spectrum regions, in this section, we assume
n
and
\lambda
satisfy

\displaystyle\mbox{{(\bf H2)}}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ n\geq 4\ \ \ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \frac{n(n-2)}{4}<\lambda<\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}.\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \

Since
p=2^{\star}-1
is fixed in this section, for the simplicity of notations we denote
S_{p,\lambda}
by
S_{\lambda},
and the unique positive radial solution of (
1.4
) will be denoted by
\mathcal{U}.
To begin with we recall the uniqueness result of
[
41
]
in its simplest form.

Theorem A

(Theorem
1.3
of
[
41
]
)
.

Assume
(H2)
, then all the positive solutions of (
1.4
) are of the form
\mathcal{U}\circ\tau_{b}
for some
b\in\mathbb{B}^{n}.

Also recall that all the positive solutions to (
1.4
) have the same energy
I_{\lambda}(\mathcal{U}\circ\tau_{b})=\frac{1}{n}S_{\lambda}^{\frac{n}{2}}.

Below are a few applications of the above existence and uniqueness of solutions to (
1.4
).

Define

\mathbcal{h}:[\frac{n(n-2)}{4},\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}]\to(0,\infty)
by
\mathbcal{h}(\lambda)=S_{\lambda}.
Clearly
\mathbcal{h}
is monotonically decreasing. We have

Lemma 3.1
.

The function
\mathbcal{h}
is strictly decreasing.

Proof.

It is well known that
S_{\frac{n(n-2)}{4}}=S
where recall
S
is the best Euclidean Sobolev constant and
S_{\lambda}<S
for
\lambda>\frac{n(n-2)}{4}
(see
[
54
]
). So, with out loss of generality we assume
\frac{n(n-2)}{4}<\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}\leq\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}
and we claim to show that
S_{\lambda_{1}}>S_{\lambda_{2}}.

On the contrary we assume
S_{\lambda_{1}}=S_{\lambda_{2}}.
Then
S_{\lambda_{1}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is also attained by
\mathcal{U}_{\lambda_{2}}
and hence by uniqueness Theorem
A
,
\mathcal{U}_{\lambda_{1}}=C\mathcal{U}_{\lambda_{2}}
for some positive constant
C.
Because of the assumed normalization (
1.3
) and
S_{\lambda_{1}}=S_{\lambda_{2}},
we conclude
C=1.
But
\mathcal{U}_{\lambda_{i}}
satisfies the Euler-Lagrange (
1.4
) with
\lambda=\lambda_{i},i=1,2.
Subtracting the two equations satisfied by
\mathcal{U}_{\lambda_{i}}
we see that
(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})\mathcal{U}_{\lambda_{1}}=0
which implies
\mathcal{U}_{\lambda_{1}}\equiv 0,
a contradiction. Hence the proof follows.
∎

A direct application of Lemma
3.1
is the following corollary. Set

(3.1)

\displaystyle\Lambda=\left\{\lambda\in\left[\frac{n(n-2)}{4},\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}\right]\ :\ \left(\frac{S_{\lambda}}{S}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}\in[0,1]\cap\mathbb{Q}\right\}.

Corollary 3.1
.

The set
\Lambda
is at most countable.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1
, the function
g(\lambda)=\left(\frac{S_{\lambda}}{S}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}=\left(\frac{\mathbcal{h}(\lambda)}{S}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}

is strictly decreasing and hence there exists a one-to-one correspondence between
\left[\frac{n(n-2)}{4},\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}\right]
and the image of
g
denoted by
Im(g).
As a result, we can write

\displaystyle\Lambda=g^{-1}(\mathbb{Q}\cap Im(g))

and since
\mathbb{Q}\cap Im(g)
is countable and
g
is strictly decreasing
\Lambda
must be countable.
∎

On the absence of Aubin-Talenti bubbles in the profile decomposition:
Recall that under the hypothesis
(H2)
the profile decomposition may contain both Aubin-Talenti bubbles and the hyperbolic bubbles. The following simple consequence of
Lemma
3.1
and Theorem
C
isolate the situation preferable for us.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
n\geq 4,p=2^{\star}-1
and
\frac{n(n-2)}{4}<\lambda<\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}
be such that
\lambda\not\in\Lambda
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
be given.
Let
\{u_{m}\}\subset H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
be a sequence such that
u_{m}\geq 0,

I_{\lambda}(u_{m})\rightarrow d=\frac{N}{n}S_{\lambda}^{\frac{n}{2}}
and

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u_{m}+\lambda u_{m}+u_{m}^{p}\|_{H^{-1}}\rightarrow 0,\ \ \mbox{as}\ \ m\rightarrow\infty.

Then there exists
N
sequences
\{b_{m}^{j}\}\subset\mathbb{B}^{n},1\leq j\leq N
such that

\displaystyle u_{m}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathcal{U}\circ\tau_{b_{m}^{j}}+o(1)\ \ in\ H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}).

In other words, no Aubin-Talenti bubble exists in the profile decomposition of
u_{m}.

Proof.

By Theorem
C
and the subsequent Remark
1.2
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{N}{n}S_{\lambda}^{\frac{n}{2}}=\frac{\tilde{N}_{1}}{n}S_{\lambda}^{\frac{n}{2}}+\frac{N_{2}}{n}S^{\frac{n}{2}}.

If
N_{2}\neq 0,
(which corresponds to the existence of Aubin-Talenti bubbles) then
N\neq\tilde{N}_{1}
and hence

\displaystyle\left(\frac{S_{\lambda}}{S}\right)^{\frac{n}{2}}=\frac{N_{2}}{N-\tilde{N}_{1}}\in\mathbb{Q}.

This contradicts the assumption that
\lambda\not\in\Lambda
and completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

At this point, we are tempted to conjecture that Theorem
3.1
is the best possible scenario we can get for
\frac{n(n-2)}{4}<\lambda<\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}.
In other words, if
\mathbcal{h}
is continuous then
Im(g)
must contain both rational and irrational numbers and thence we can not expect to do better than Theorem
3.1
. However, proving rigorously the existence of both Aubin-Talenti bubbles and hyperbolic bubbles at the prescribed energy level mentioned in Theorem
3.1
is a formidable challenge. We don’t have any positive or negative answer to it yet.

4.
Interaction of bubbles

4.1.
Interaction of two bubbles

In this subsection, we shall compute the interaction of two hyperbolic bubbles. We use the following sharp bounds on the hyperbolic bubble: there exist constants
\chi_{i}=\chi_{i}(n,p,\lambda),\,i=1,2
such that

(4.1)

\displaystyle\chi_{1}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,z)}\leq\mathcal{U}[z](x)\leq\chi_{2}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,z)},\ \ \ \mbox{for all}\ \ x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},

and for every
z\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
, where
c(n,\lambda)
is as defined in (
1.10
). The case
\lambda=0
and
p<2^{\star}-1
the decay estimate (
4.1
) have been obtained by Bonforte-Gazzola-Grillo and Vázquez
[
11
]
some time ago which is a slight improvement of the seminal result of Mancini-Sandeep
[
41
]
concerning the asymptotic behaviour of radial solutions.
We came to know about the validity of (
4.1
) for all admissible
\lambda
from personal communication with Sandeep K and Ramya Dutta
[
48
]
. We remark that this article was completed only after knowing the results of Sandeep and Ramya, and we thank them for explaining and sharing the estimates to us. The result is going to appear in their forthcoming article.

Lemma 4.1
.

Given
n\geq 3,
let
\alpha,\beta\geq 0
be such that
\alpha+\beta\geq 2.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}[z_{i}],i=1,2
be two hyperbolic bubbles such that
d(z_{i},z_{j})\geq\ln 3.
Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\beta}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\ \begin{cases}\approx_{|\alpha-\beta|}\ e^{-c(n,\lambda)\min\{\alpha,\beta\}\,d(z_{1},z_{2})},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ \alpha\neq\beta\\
\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\approx\ \ \ \ \ \ d(z_{1},z_{2})e^{-c(n,\lambda)\beta\,d(z_{1},z_{2})},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ \alpha=\beta.\\
\end{cases}

Here we follow the convention that the constant in
\approx
depends also on
n,p
and
\lambda.

Proof.

Before beginning the proof let us recall that for any two points
x,z\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
we have

\displaystyle\cosh\frac{d(x,z)}{2}\,=\,\dfrac{\sqrt{1-2x.z+|x|^{2}|z|^{2}}}{\sqrt{(1-|z|^{2})(1-|x|^{2})}}.

So in particular for
z=0
we have

\displaystyle e^{d(x,0)}\,\approx\,\dfrac{1}{(1-|x|^{2})}.

By Lemma
2.1
(iii) it is enough to prove the result for
z_{1}=0,z_{2}=z.

Let us compute the following integral for
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\}
such that
\alpha+\beta\geq 2:

\displaystyle I\,

\displaystyle=\,\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\alpha}\,\mathcal{U}[z]^{\beta}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\approx\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}e^{-c(n,\lambda)\alpha d(x,0)}e^{-c(n,\lambda)\beta d(x,z)}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\approx\int_{\mathsf{B}^{n}}(1-|x|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)\alpha}\left(\frac{(1-|z|^{2})(1-|x|^{2})}{1-2x.z+|x|^{2}|z|^{2}}\right)^{c(n,\lambda)\beta}\,\left(\frac{2}{1-|x|^{2}}\right)^{n}\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\approx(1-|z|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)\beta}\int_{\mathsf{B}^{n}}\frac{(1-|x|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)(\alpha+\beta)-n}}{(1-2x.z+|x|^{2}|z|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)\beta}}\,{\rm d}x

(4.2)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)\beta}}{|z|^{2c(n,\lambda)\beta}}\int_{\mathsf{B}^{n}}\frac{(1-|x|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)(\alpha+\beta)-n}}{|x-z^{*}|^{2c(n,\lambda)\beta}}\,{\rm d}x.

Note that the the values of
c(n,\lambda)>\frac{(n-1)}{2}
for all
\lambda<\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}
and hence the integral (
4.1
) makes sense only if
\alpha+\beta\geq 2.

Now without loss of generality, we may assume that
\alpha\geq\beta.
We further subdivide our proof into two cases.

Case-1:
\alpha>\beta.
We notice that
|z^{*}|\geq 1
and
|x|<1
and therefore

\displaystyle|x-z^{*}|\geq|z^{*}|-|x|\geq 1-|x|\approx(1-|x|^{2}).

Since by our hypothesis
d(0,z)\geq\ln 3,
we have
|z|\geq\frac{1}{2}
and hence
\mathsf{B}^{n}\subset B_{E}(z^{*},3).
Exploiting the fact that
c(n,\lambda)(\alpha-\beta)>0
we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathsf{B}^{n}}\frac{(1-|x|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)(\alpha+\beta)-n}}{|x-z^{*}|^{2c(n,\lambda)\beta}}\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathsf{B}^{n}}|x-z^{*}|^{c(n,\lambda)(\alpha-\beta)-n}\,{\rm d}x

(4.3)

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{B_{E}(z^{*},3)}|x-z^{*}|^{c(n,\lambda)(\alpha-\beta)-n}\,{\rm d}x\lesssim_{|\alpha-\beta|}1.

This proves the upper bound.

For the lower bound, first note that for fixed
z_{0}
with
\frac{1}{2}\leq|z_{0}|\leq 1
the same argument leads to

\displaystyle 0<\int_{\mathsf{B}^{n}}\frac{(1-|x|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)(\alpha+\beta)-n}}{|x-z_{0}^{*}|^{2c(n,\lambda)\beta}}\,{\rm d}x\lesssim_{|\alpha-\beta|}1.

Taking a minimizing sequence and using Fatou’s lemma one can easily conclude that

\displaystyle\inf_{\frac{1}{2}\leq|z|\leq 1}\int_{\mathsf{B}^{n}}\frac{(1-|x|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)(\alpha+\beta)-n}}{|x-z^{*}|^{2c(n,\lambda)\beta}}\,{\rm d}x\approx_{|\alpha-\beta|}1.

This completes the proof lower bound. Hence combining the upper bound and lower bound estimates we conclude that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\alpha}\,\mathcal{U}[z]^{\beta}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\approx_{|\alpha-\beta|}(1-|z|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)\beta}\approx_{|\alpha-\beta|}e^{-c(n,\lambda)\mbox{min}\{\alpha,\beta\}d(0,z)}.

Case-2:
\alpha=\beta.

The upper estimate.

In this case, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathsf{B}^{n}}\frac{(1-|x|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)(\alpha+\beta)-n}}{|x-z^{*}|^{2c(n,\lambda)\beta}}\,{\rm d}x\lesssim\int_{\mathsf{B}^{n}}\frac{1}{|x-z^{*}|^{n}}\,{\rm d}x

In the proof all constants depend on
n,\lambda
and
p.
With out loss of generality we may assume that
z^{*}=|z^{*}|e_{n}=te_{n},
where
t=\frac{1}{|z|}.
Now we transformation the integral in polar co-ordinates

\displaystyle x_{1}=\varrho\,\sin\theta\sin\theta_{1}\ldots\sin\theta_{n-3}\sin\theta_{n-2}

\displaystyle x_{2}=\varrho\,\sin\theta\sin\theta_{1}\ldots\sin\theta_{n-3}\cos\theta_{n-2}

\displaystyle x_{3}=\varrho\,\sin\theta\sin\theta_{1}\ldots\cos\theta_{n-3}

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle x_{n}=\varrho\cos\theta.

The Jacobian of the above transformation is
\rho^{n-1}\sin^{n-2}\theta\sin^{n-3}\theta_{1}\cdots\sin\theta_{n-3},

where
\rho\in[0,1],

\theta,\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n-3}\in[0,\pi]
and
\theta_{n-2}\in[0,2\pi].
With the above transformation, our integral now transforms to

\displaystyle\int_{\mathsf{B}^{n}}\frac{1}{|x-z^{*}|^{n}}\,{\rm d}x\,

\displaystyle\approx\,\int_{0}^{1}\varrho^{n-1}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\sin^{n-2}\theta\ {\rm d}\theta}{(t^{2}-2t\varrho\cos\theta+\varrho^{2})^{n/2}}\,{\rm d}\varrho

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{|z|}\varrho^{n-1}{\rm d}\varrho\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\sin^{n-2}\theta\ {\rm d}\theta}{(1-2\varrho\cos\theta+\varrho^{2})^{n/2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{|z|}\frac{\varrho^{n-1}}{\varrho^{n}}{\rm d}\varrho\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\sin^{n-2}\theta\ {\rm d}\theta}{(1/\varrho^{2}-2/\varrho\cos\theta+1)^{n/2}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{|z|}\frac{\varrho^{n-1}}{\varrho^{n}}\frac{\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{n-2}\theta\ {\rm d}\theta}{(1/\varrho)^{n-2}(1/\varrho^{2}-1)}{\rm d}\varrho

\displaystyle=C\int_{0}^{|z|}\frac{\varrho^{n-1}}{1-\varrho^{2}}\,{\rm d}\varrho,

where in the first line we have absorbed the term
2\pi\prod_{l=1}^{n-3}\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{l}\theta\ d\theta
into
\approx
sign and in the second line we have made the change of variable
\varrho\to t\varrho
. In the last line
C:=\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{n-2}\theta\ {\rm d}\theta
and in the fourth line we have used the well-known formula

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\sin^{n-2}\theta\ {\rm d}\theta}{(r^{2}-2r\cos\theta+1)^{n/2}}\,{\rm d}\theta=\frac{1}{r^{n-2}(r^{2}-1)}\int_{0}^{\pi}\sin^{n-2}\theta\ {\rm d}\theta,\ \ \mbox{for}\ r>1.

Further, we estimate using
t=\frac{1}{|z|}\approx 1
as follows

(4.4)

\displaystyle\frac{C}{t}\int_{0}^{|z|}\frac{\varrho^{n-1}}{1-\varrho^{2}}\,{\rm d}\varrho\lesssim|z|^{n-2}\int_{0}^{|z|}\frac{\varrho}{1-\varrho^{2}}\,{\rm d}\varrho\lesssim\log\left(\frac{1}{1-|z|^{2}}\right)\approx d(0,z).

Substituting (
4.4
) in (
4.1
) we obtain the desired upper bound

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\alpha}\,\mathcal{U}[z]^{\beta}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim d(0,z)e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(0,z)}=d(0,z)e^{-c(n,\lambda)\mbox{min}\{\alpha,\beta\}d(0,z)}.

The lower estimate.
Now we shall compute the lower bound. We fix
\theta_{0}
small to be determined later (actually,
\theta_{0}=\pi/4
will work). We consider the following cone defined by the collection of all
x\in B_{E}(z,|z|)
such that the angle between
(x-z)
and
-z
is less than or equal to
\theta_{0}.

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}:=\{x\in B_{E}(z,|z|):-(x-z)\cdot z\geq|x-z||z|\,\cos\theta_{0}\},

where recall
\cdot
denotes the standard Euclidean inner product. We emphasise that we shall choose
\theta_{0}
independent of the point
z.

\mathcal{C}
z*
\tiny\theta_{0}
x
z

Figure 1
.
The above picture demonstrating the bounded cone
\mathcal{C}
where the terms
(1-|x|^{2})
and
|x-z^{*}|
are comparable for all
x\in\mathcal{C}.

First we claim that if
|z|\sim 1,
then

\displaystyle|x-z^{*}|\lesssim(1-|x|^{2})\quad\ \ \mbox{for all}\ x\in\mathcal{C}.

Proof of the claim:
By triangle inequality

(4.5)

\displaystyle|x-z^{*}|\leq|x-z|+|z-z^{*}|\leq|x-z|+\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{|z|}\lesssim|x-z|+(1-|z|^{2}).

On the other hand
|x|^{2}=|x-z|^{2}+2(x-z)\cdot z+|z|^{2},
and hence

\displaystyle 1-|x|^{2}

\displaystyle=1-|z|^{2}-|x-z|^{2}-2(x-z)\cdot z\geq(1-|z|^{2})-|x-z|^{2}+2|x-z||z|\cos\theta_{0}

(4.6)

\displaystyle=(1-|z|^{2})+|x-z|\left(2|z|\cos\theta_{0}-|x-z|\right).

In the
\mathcal{C},
we have
|x-z|\leq|z|,
therefore

\displaystyle 2|z|\cos\theta_{0}-|x-z|\geq|z|(2\cos\theta_{0}-1)\geq(\sqrt{2}-1)|z|

if
\theta_{0}=\pi/4.
We get from (
4.1
)

(4.7)

\displaystyle 1-|x|^{2}\geq(\sqrt{2}-1)\left[(1-|z|^{2})+|x-z|\right].

Hence from (
4.5
) and (
4.7
) the claim follows. Now given the above, we simplify the required integral as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{(1-|x|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)(\alpha+\beta)-n}}{|x-z^{*}|^{2c(n,\lambda)\beta}}\,{\rm d}x\gtrsim\int_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{1}{|x-z^{*}|^{n}}\,{\rm d}x.

Now we again make the following change of variable in polar co-ordinate

\displaystyle x_{1}=-\varrho\,\sin\theta\sin\theta_{1}\ldots\sin\theta_{n-3}\sin\theta_{n-2}

\displaystyle x_{2}=-\varrho\,\sin\theta\sin\theta_{1}\ldots\sin\theta_{n-3}\cos\theta_{n-2}

\displaystyle x_{3}=-\varrho\,\sin\theta\sin\theta_{1}\ldots\cos\theta_{n-3}

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle x_{n}=|z|-\varrho\cos\theta,\quad z=|z|e_{n},\ t=\frac{1}{|z|}.

With the above change of variable, we can write

\displaystyle|x-z^{*}|^{2}

\displaystyle=|x|^{2}+|z^{*}|^{2}-2x_{n}z_{n}^{*}=\varrho^{2}+|z|^{2}-2|z|\varrho\cos\theta+\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}-2\left(|z|-\varrho\cos\theta\right)\frac{1}{|z|}

\displaystyle=\varrho^{2}+2\varrho\cos\theta\left(\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{|z|}\right)+\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{2}}{|z|^{2}}.

This yields for
|z|\approx 1

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{C}}\frac{1}{|x-z^{*}|^{n}}\,{\rm d}x

\displaystyle\approx\int_{0}^{|z|}\varrho^{n-1}\,{\rm d}\varrho\int_{0}^{\theta_{0}}\frac{(\sin\theta)^{n-2}\,{\rm d}\theta}{\big[\varrho^{2}+2\varrho\cos\theta\left(\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{|z|}\right)+\frac{(1-|z|^{2})^{2}}{|z|^{2}}\big]^{\frac{n}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\frac{|z|^{2}}{1-|z|^{2}}}\dfrac{\left(\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{|z|}\varrho\right)^{n-1}}{\left(\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{|z|}\right)^{n-1}}\,{\rm d}\varrho\int_{0}^{\theta_{0}}\frac{(\sin\theta)^{n-2}\,{\rm d}\theta}{\big[\varrho^{2}+2\varrho\cos\theta+1\big]^{\frac{n}{2}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\frac{|z|^{2}}{1-|z|^{2}}}\varrho^{n-1}{\rm d}\varrho\int_{0}^{\theta_{0}}\frac{(\sin\theta)^{n-2}\,{\rm d}\theta}{\big[\varrho^{2}+2\varrho\cos\theta+1\big]^{\frac{n}{2}}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim\int_{1}^{\frac{|z|^{2}}{1-|z|^{2}}}\varrho^{n-1}\dfrac{1}{\varrho^{n}}\int_{0}^{\theta_{0}}\sin^{n-2}\theta\ {\rm d}\theta\,{\rm d}\varrho

\displaystyle\gtrsim C_{\theta_{0}}\int_{1}^{\frac{|z|^{2}}{1-|z|^{2}}}\frac{{\rm d}\varrho}{\varrho}\gtrsim\log\left(\frac{|z|^{2}}{1-|z|^{2}}\right)\gtrsim\log\left(\frac{1}{1-|z|^{2}}\right)

(4.8)

\displaystyle\approx d(0,z)

where in the second line we used the change of variable
\varrho\to\left(\frac{1-|z|^{2}}{|z|}\right)\varrho.

Therefore from the above computation, we conclude

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\alpha}\,\mathcal{U}[z]^{\beta}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\gtrsim d(0,z)e^{-c(n,\lambda)\beta d(0,z)}.

∎

Remark 4.1
.

The sharpness of the exponent in Lemma
4.1
in case
\alpha\neq\beta
can also be seen from the following heuristic explanation. Note that

\displaystyle e^{d(x,z)-d(0,z)}\approx\frac{1-2x.z+|x|^{2}|z|^{2}}{1-|x|^{2}}.

Then as
|z|\rightarrow 1,
i.e.
z\rightarrow e
with
|e|=1,
there holds

\displaystyle e^{d(x,z)-d(0,z)}\rightarrow\frac{1-2x\cdot e+|x|^{2}}{1-|x|^{2}}\approx|x-e|^{2}e^{d(0,x)}

Therefore for
\alpha>\beta,
denoting
c=c(n,\lambda)

\displaystyle e^{c\beta d(0,z)}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}e^{-c\alpha d(0,x)}e^{-c\beta d(x,z)}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(x)\rightarrow

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}e^{-c\alpha d(0,x)}|x-e|^{-2c\beta}e^{-c\beta d(0,x)}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(x)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\int_{\mathsf{B}^{n}}\frac{(1-|x|^{2})^{c(\alpha+\beta)-n}}{|x-e|^{2c\beta}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(x)\approx 1.

By replacing
\beta
in
e^{c\beta d(0,z)}
by
\nu,
we observe that as
|z|\rightarrow 1,

\displaystyle e^{c\nu d(0,z)}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}e^{-c\alpha d(0,x)}e^{-c\beta d(x,z)}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(x)\rightarrow\begin{cases}0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ \nu<\beta\\
+\infty\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ \nu>\beta.\\
\end{cases}

This proves that
\nu=\beta
is the precise asymptotic estimate for the interaction terms.

4.2.
Interaction of three bubbles

In this section, we will derive the interaction of three hyperbolic bubbles. We denote
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}[z_{i}],\ i=1,2,3.
The main focus of this section is to derive upper bounds on the integral of interactions.
We recall

\displaystyle Q_{ij}:=e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z_{i},z_{j})}\ \ \ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \ \ Q:=\max_{i\neq j}\ Q_{ij}.

We prove the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}[z_{i}],i=1,2,3
be three hyperbolic bubbles and
p>1
and assume
Q<<1
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{2}\mathcal{U}_{3}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\ \begin{cases}\ \lesssim\ Q^{\frac{3}{2}}\left(\ln\frac{1}{Q}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ p-1>1,\\
\ \lesssim\ Q^{\frac{3}{2}}\ln\frac{1}{Q},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ p-1=1,\\
\ \lesssim_{\nu}\ Q^{\nu},\ \mbox{for any}\ \nu<\frac{p+1}{2},\ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ p-1<1.\end{cases}

Proof.

p-1>1.
We apply Hölder inequality as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{2}\mathcal{U}_{3}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}})(\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{3}^{\frac{1}{2}})(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{3}^{\frac{1}{2}})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\frac{3(p-1)}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\frac{3}{2}}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\frac{3}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{3}^{\frac{3}{2}}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\frac{3(p-1)}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{3}^{\frac{3}{2}}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim Q_{12}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(Q_{23}^{\frac{3}{2}}\ln\frac{1}{Q_{23}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}Q_{13}^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim Q^{\frac{3}{2}}\left(\ln\frac{1}{Q}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}},

where we have used
t\to t^{\epsilon\ln(1/t)}
is increasing near
t=0.

p-1=1.
The idea is the same as in case (i). We apply Hölder inequality as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{1}\mathcal{U}_{2}\mathcal{U}_{3}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}})(\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{3}^{\frac{1}{2}})(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{3}^{\frac{1}{2}})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\frac{3}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\frac{3}{2}}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\frac{3}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{3}^{\frac{3}{2}}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\frac{3}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{3}^{\frac{3}{2}}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(Q_{12}^{\frac{3}{2}}\ln\frac{1}{Q_{12}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\left(Q_{23}^{\frac{3}{2}}\ln\frac{1}{Q_{23}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\left(Q_{13}^{\frac{3}{2}}\ln\frac{1}{Q_{13}}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim Q^{\frac{3}{2}}\ln\frac{1}{Q}.

We fix
\beta>\alpha>1
and
s_{1},s_{2}\in(0,1)
that to be decided later and apply Hölder

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{2}\mathcal{U}_{3}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\beta}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)^{s_{1}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\alpha}\mathcal{U}_{3}^{\beta}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)^{s_{1}}\left(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{2}^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{3}^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)^{s_{2}}.

A quick sanity check gives

(4.9)

\displaystyle 2\alpha s_{1}=p-1,\ \ \ \beta s_{1}+\frac{p+1}{2}s_{2}=1.

Since we assumed
\alpha<\beta,
the interaction estimates of the previous section gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{1}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{2}\mathcal{U}_{3}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq Q_{12}^{\alpha s_{1}}Q_{13}^{\alpha s_{1}}\left(Q_{23}^{\frac{p+1}{2}}\ln\frac{1}{Q_{23}}\right)^{s_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Q^{2\alpha s_{1}+\frac{p+1}{2}s_{2}}\left(\ln\frac{1}{Q}\right)^{s_{2}}

\displaystyle=Q^{p-\beta s_{1}}\left(\ln\frac{1}{Q}\right)^{s_{2}}.

If we want the interaction to be
o(Q)
then we need
p-\beta s_{1}>1.
Now let us check that all the above requirements are feasible. Choose
\epsilon>0
small and set
\alpha=\frac{(p+1)(1-\epsilon)}{2},\beta=\frac{(p+1)(1+\epsilon)}{2}.
Then (
4.9
) gives for sufficiently small
\epsilon

\displaystyle s_{1}=\frac{p-1}{(p+1)(1-\epsilon)}<1,\ \ \beta s_{1}=\frac{(1+\epsilon)(p-1)}{2(1-\epsilon)}<p-1,

\displaystyle\frac{p+1}{2}s_{1}=1-\beta s_{1}=\frac{3-\epsilon-(1+\epsilon)p}{2(1-\epsilon)}<\frac{p+1}{2}.

Next we show that for any
\nu<\frac{p+1}{2}
we can choose
\epsilon>0
such that
\nu<p-\beta s_{1}.
Indeed, direct computation gives

\displaystyle p-\beta s_{1}=p-\frac{(1+\epsilon)(p-1)}{2(1-\epsilon)}\rightarrow\frac{p+1}{2},\ \ \mbox{as}\ \epsilon\rightarrow 0

and hence we can choose such
\epsilon.
As a result

\displaystyle Q^{p-\beta s_{1}}\left(\ln\frac{1}{Q}\right)^{s_{2}}=Q^{\nu}Q^{p-\beta s_{1}-\nu}\left(\ln\frac{1}{Q}\right)^{s_{2}}\lesssim_{\nu}Q^{\nu}

if
Q<<1.
This completes the proof.

∎

Remark 4.2
.

The constant in the third case i.e
p-1<1
depends on
\nu.
In our first case if
p-1>\frac{3}{2}
then we can refine the upper bound to
Q^{\min\{p-1,2\}}.
Since our interest is in
p\leq 2^{\star}-1
such assumptions are void in dimension
n\geq 5.
The proof follows in the same manner by clubbing
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{2}\mathcal{U}_{3}=\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{2}\right)\left(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\mathcal{U}_{3}\right)

and using Hölder inequality.

4.3.
Interaction of bubbles and the derivative of bubbles

In this section, we find the estimates on the interaction of bubbles and the space derivatives of other bubbles. Before proceeding let us first simplify the formula of the derivative. In coordinates
\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}(\mathcal{U}\circ\tau_{te_{j}})=V_{j}(\mathcal{U}),
where
