V_{j}=(1+|x|^{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}-2x_{j}\sum_{l=1}^{n}x_{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{l}}
and the derivatives involved are in the sense of Euclidean (see Appendix
A
).

Recall the Euclidean norm
r=|x|
and the hyperbolic distance
\rho=d(x,0)
is related by
r=\tanh\frac{\rho}{2}.
Since a hyperbolic ball with center
0
is also a Euclidean ball with center
0
but possibly different radius, we deduce
\mathcal{U}
is a function of
r.
By abuse of notation we denote
\mathcal{U}(x)=\mathcal{U}(\rho)=U_{1}(r).
Then direct computation gives

(4.10)

\displaystyle V_{j}(\mathcal{U})=\frac{x_{j}}{r}(1-r^{2})\frac{d}{dr}(U_{1}(r))=2\frac{x_{j}}{|x|}\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(\rho)

where
\prime
denotes the derivative with respect to
\rho
and recall
\rho=d(x,0).

It follows directly from the above expression and the bound
|\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(\rho)|\lesssim\mathcal{U}(\rho)
that
|V_{j}(\mathcal{U})|\lesssim\mathcal{U}
on
\mathbb{B}^{n}.
Moreover, note that as
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}<0
for all
\rho>0,

V_{j}(\mathcal{U})
changes sign in
\mathbb{B}^{n}.

We prove the following

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
does not lie on the hyperplane
P_{j}:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |\ x_{j}=0\}.
Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[0])\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\approx_{z}\begin{cases}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z,0)},\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ z_{j}<0,\\
-e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z,0)},\ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ z_{j}>0,\\
\end{cases}

where
\approx_{z}
indicates the constant depends on (the position of)
z.

Remark 4.3
.

Before proving the lemma let us first remark why it should be true. Also, this is a good time to remark that this is in sharp contrast to the Euclidean case. In the previous section, we observed that the interaction is an exponentially decaying function of the distance between the points where the bubbles are most concentrated. The derivative of an exponential function is an exponential function while the derivative of the distance function is of the absolute value of approximately 1 and that precisely plays the decisive role here. Formally,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[0])\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]^{p}\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\mathcal{U}[te_{j}]\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]^{p}\mathcal{U}[te_{j}]\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z,te_{j})}

\displaystyle\approx e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z,0)}\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}d(z,te_{j})

\displaystyle\approx_{z}\pm e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z,0)}.

On the other hand, in the Euclidean case, the interaction of Aubin-Talenti bubbles has polynomial decay:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U[z,1]^{p}U[z_{1},1]\ dx\approx|z-z_{1}|^{-(n-2)}

and hence

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U[z,1]^{p}\partial_{x_{j}}U[0,1]\ dx\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}U[z,1]^{p}U[te_{j},1]\ dx\Big|

\displaystyle\approx\Big|\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}|z-te_{j}|^{-(n-2)}\Big|

\displaystyle\approx|z|^{-(n-1)}<<|z|^{-(n-2)}\ \ \mbox{if}\ \ |z|>>1.

See the treatise by A. Bahri
[
3
, Estimate (F11)]
. However, the interaction of bubbles and the
\mu
-derivative of another bubble has the same decay as the interaction of bubbles if the height of the bubbles is comparable
[
3
, Estimate (F16)]
.

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

Proof.

First, we simplify the expression as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[0])\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z])V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[0])\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z](-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[0]))\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=p\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]\mathcal{U}[0]^{p-1}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[0])\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

(4.11)

\displaystyle=2p\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]\mathcal{U}[0]^{p-1}\frac{x_{j}}{|x|}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\prime}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

Recall that
\mathcal{U}[0]^{\prime}\leq 0
on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
and radial and hence the term
\frac{x_{j}}{|x|}
will play the central role in our estimate. Also, note that the upper bound follows directly from the bound
|\mathcal{U}^{\prime}(\rho)|\lesssim\mathcal{U}(\rho)
and the estimates on the interaction of bubbles obtained in the last subsection.

For
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},
we denote by
\hat{x}
the reflection of
x
with respect to the plane
P_{j}.

We denote by
\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}
the negative half of the ball
\{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\ |\ x_{j}<0\}.

\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}
is similarly defined with
<0
replaced by
>0.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
z\in\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}.

First, we claim that

Claim:

d(x,z)\leq d(\hat{x},z)
for all
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}.

The proof follows from the formula of the hyperbolic distance.

\displaystyle\cosh^{2}\left(\frac{d(\hat{x},z)}{2}\right)-\cosh^{2}\left(\frac{d(x,z)}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2(x-\hat{x})\cdot z}{(1-|x|^{2})(1-|z|^{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{2x_{j}z_{j}}{(1-|x|^{2})(1-|z|^{2})}>0

as both
x,z\in\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}.
Since
\cosh
is strictly increasing the claim follows. Since
\mathcal{U}[z]
is strictly decreasing in
d(x,z)
we deduce
\mathcal{U}[z](x)>\mathcal{U}[z](\hat{x})
for all
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}.

We further simplify the integral
(
4.3
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[0])\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=2p\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]\mathcal{U}[0]^{p-1}\frac{x_{j}}{|x|}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\prime}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=2p\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}}\mathcal{U}[z]\mathcal{U}[0]^{p-1}\frac{x_{j}}{|x|}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\prime}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+2p\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}}\mathcal{U}[z]\mathcal{U}[0]^{p-1}\frac{x_{j}}{|x|}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\prime}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

(4.12)

\displaystyle=2p\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}}(\mathcal{U}[z](x)-\mathcal{U}[z](\hat{x}))\mathcal{U}[0]^{p-1}(x)\frac{x_{j}}{|x|}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\prime}(x)\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(x).

Notice that the integrand in (
4.3
) is non-negative. The proof can be finished if we show that
\mathcal{U}[z](x)-\mathcal{U}[z](\hat{x})\gtrsim\mathcal{U}[z](x)
on a compact subdomain
B
of
\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}.
We achieve this by comparison principle.
Let us denote
w(x)=\mathcal{U}[z](x)-\mathcal{U}[z](\hat{x})
and fix a compact domain
B\subset\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}.
Then
w
satisfies

(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda)w=\mathcal{U}[z]^{p}(x)-\mathcal{U}[z]^{p}(\hat{x})\geq p\mathcal{U}[z]^{p-1}(x)w(x)\geq\mathcal{U}[z]^{p-1}(x)w(x)

as
w\geq 0
in
B.
Let
c_{1}=\inf_{x\in\partial B}w(x)>0
and let
c_{2}
be such that
c_{2}\sup_{x\in\partial B}\mathcal{U}[z](x)\leq c_{1}.
Note that the constants
c_{1},c_{2}
depends on
B
and hence on the location of
z.
Since
c_{2}\mathcal{U}[z]
satisfies
(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda)(c_{2}\mathcal{U}[z])=\mathcal{U}[z]^{p-1}(c_{2}\mathcal{U}[z])
, weak comparison principle gives
w\geq c_{2}\mathcal{U}[z]
in
B.
Indeed, denoting
v(x)=w(x)-c_{2}\mathcal{U}[z](x),
we see that
v
satisfies

(4.13)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}v-\lambda v\geq\mathcal{U}[z]^{p-1}v,\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{in}\ B,\\
\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ v\geq 0\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{on}\ \partial B.\end{cases}

Multiplying (
4.13
) by
v^{-}\in H_{0}^{1}(B)
and integrating by parts gives

(4.14)

\displaystyle\int_{B}(|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}v^{-}|^{2}-\lambda|v^{-}|^{2})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\int_{B}\mathcal{U}[z]^{p-1}|v^{-}|^{2}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

But (
4.14
) is possible only when
v^{-}=0
because the first Dirichlet eigenvalue of the operator

(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda)/\mathcal{U}[z]^{p-1}
on
\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}
is
p>1
(see Lemma
A.1
) and so on
B
it must be bigger than
1.
Hence

v\geq 0
on
B.

Moreover, if the compact set stays away from the hyperplane
\{x_{j}=0\}
then we have the lower bound
\frac{x_{j}}{|x|}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\prime}(x)\gtrsim\mathcal{U}[0]
on
B.
Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[0])

\displaystyle=2p\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}}(\mathcal{U}[z](x)-\mathcal{U}[z](\hat{x}))\mathcal{U}[0]^{p-1}(x)\frac{x_{j}}{|x|}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\prime}(x)\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(x)

\displaystyle\geq 2pc_{2}\int_{B}\mathcal{U}[z]\mathcal{U}[0]^{p-1}(x)\frac{x_{j}}{|x|}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\prime}(x)\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(x)

\displaystyle\gtrsim_{z}\int_{B}\mathcal{U}[z]\mathcal{U}[0]^{p}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\gtrsim_{z}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z,0)}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

Note that we can not do better than
\approx_{z},
i.e., the constant must depend on
z.
To view this note that
if
z_{j}=0
then it follows from the formula of the hyperbolic distance that
d(x,z)=d(\hat{x},z)
where recall
\hat{x}
is the reflection of
x
with respect to the plane
\{x_{j}=0\}.
As a result, the interaction vanishes and hence the constant must depend on the position of
z.
Indeed, in the next lemma we show that if
z_{j}
stays away from 0, then we can make the bound uniform in the sense that if
|z_{j}|\geq\kappa>0
then the constant depends only on
\kappa.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
satisfy
|z_{j}|\geq\kappa
for some constant
\kappa\in(0,1).
Then there exists a
\delta_{0}>0
such that for all
z
satisfying
1-|z|<\delta_{0}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[0])\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\approx_{k,\delta_{0}}\begin{cases}e^{-cd(z,0)},\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ z_{j}<-\kappa,\\
-e^{-cd(z,0)},\ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ z_{j}>\kappa,\\
\end{cases}

where
\approx_{k,\delta_{0}}
indicates a constant that depends on
\kappa,\delta_{0}
and the parameters
n,\lambda,p.

Proof.

As in Lemma
4.3
, we may assume
z_{j}\leq-\kappa
and we only need to obtain the lower bound. Given
z
as in the statement of the lemma, we set
e=\frac{z}{|z|}.
We fix
R_{0}
large whose value will be decided later. We fix a compact set
B
(
=B_{e}
, see Figure
2
) satisfying the following two conditions:

(i)

|x-e|<\frac{1}{R_{0}}
for all
x\in B,

(ii)

The Euclidean diameter of
B

\leq\frac{\kappa}{3}
and
x_{j}\leq-\frac{\kappa}{2},
for all
x\in B.

-e_{j}
z
e=\frac{z}{|z|}
B_{e}

Figure 2
.
The above picture demonstrating the choice of the compact set
B_{e}
which depends only on the direction
e
of
z
.

Note that
B
depends only on the direction of
z.

As before we denote by
\hat{x}
the reflection of
x
with respect to the plane
\{x_{j}=0\}.
We claim that if
R_{0}
is sufficiently large and
\delta_{0}
is sufficiently small then
d(\hat{x},z)-d(x,z)
is large. We compute

\displaystyle d(\hat{x},z)-d(x,z)

\displaystyle=2\left(\ln\left(\frac{|z||\hat{x}-z^{*}|+|\hat{x}-z|}{\sqrt{(1-|x|^{2})(1-|z|^{2})}}\right)-\ln\left(\frac{|z||x-z^{*}|+|x-z|}{\sqrt{(1-|x|^{2})(1-|z|^{2})}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2\ln\left(\frac{|z||\hat{x}-z^{*}|+|\hat{x}-z|}{|z||x-z^{*}|+|x-z|}\right)

Recalling
e=\frac{z}{|z|},z_{k}\leq-\kappa
and
1-|z|<\delta_{0}
we estimate

\displaystyle|z||x-z^{*}|+|x-z|

\displaystyle\leq|z||x-e|+|z||e-z^{*}|+|x-e|+|e-z|

\displaystyle\leq(1+|z|)|x-e|+(1+|z|^{-1})(1-|z|)

\displaystyle\leq(1+\kappa^{-1})(R_{0}^{-1}+\delta_{0}).

On the other hand as
x_{k}\leq-\frac{\kappa}{2}
we see that
|z||\hat{x}-z^{*}|+|\hat{x}-z|\gtrsim\kappa
and hence

\displaystyle d(\hat{x},z)-d(x,z)\gtrsim 2\ln\left(\frac{\kappa}{(1+\kappa^{-1})(R_{0}^{-1}+\delta_{0})}\right)=:R_{1}.

Clearly, if
R_{0}
is sufficiently large and if
\delta_{0}
is sufficiently small then
R_{1}
can be made large. Now recall that there exists universal positive constants
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
such that
\chi_{1}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,z)}\leq\mathcal{U}[z](x)\leq\chi_{2}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,z)}
for all
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}.
On the boundary of the fixed ball
B,x\in\partial B
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}[z](x)-\mathcal{U}[z](\hat{x})

\displaystyle\geq\chi_{1}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,z)}-\chi_{2}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(\hat{x},z)}

\displaystyle\geq\chi_{1}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,z)}-\chi_{2}e^{-c(n,\lambda)(d(x,z)+R_{1})}

\displaystyle\geq(\chi_{1}-\chi_{2}e^{-c(n,\lambda)R_{1}})e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,z)}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{\chi_{1}-\chi_{2}e^{-c(n,\lambda)R_{1}}}{\chi_{2}}\right)\mathcal{U}[z](x).

Hence the choice of
R_{1}
such that
\chi_{1}-\chi_{2}e^{-c(n,\lambda)R_{1}}\geq\chi_{1}/2
would suffice to ensure uniform lower bound
\mathcal{U}[z](x)-\mathcal{U}[z](\hat{x})\gtrsim_{\kappa,\delta_{0}}\mathcal{U}[z](x)
on
B.
Moreover, as
x_{k}\leq-\frac{\kappa}{2}
on
B
we have
\frac{x_{j}}{|x|}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\prime}\gtrsim_{\kappa}\mathcal{U}[0]
on
B.
Hence

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}[z]^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}[0])\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=2p\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}}(\mathcal{U}[z](x)-\mathcal{U}[z](\hat{x}))\mathcal{U}[0]^{p-1}(x)\frac{x_{j}}{|x|}\mathcal{U}[0]^{\prime}(x)\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(x)

\displaystyle\gtrsim_{\kappa,\delta_{0}}\int_{B}\mathcal{U}[z]\mathcal{U}[0]^{p}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

(4.15)

\displaystyle\gtrsim_{\kappa,\delta_{0}}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z,0)}\int_{B}\frac{(1-|x|^{2})^{c(n,\lambda)(p+1)-n}}{|x-z^{*}|^{2c(n,\lambda)}}\ dx.

Note that the same
B
can be chosen for any points on the line segment
[z,e).

Finally, to conclude the proof we note that given any two points
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
satisfying the hypothesis of the lemma and
|z_{1}|=|z_{2}|
, let
A
be the orthogonal transformation that takes
z_{1}
to
z_{2}.
If
B_{1}
is the ball that is chosen for the point
z_{1}
according to the properties
(i)
and
(ii)
then
B_{2}=A(B_{1})
would satisfy the same properties for the point
z_{2}.
Hence for each such
z
we can choose
B
so that the integral involved in (
4.3
) can be bound from below uniformly with respect to
z.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

5.
Proof of the main stability theorem

In this section, we prove the main theorem of this paper, namely Theorem
1.1
.

Proof of Theorem
1.1
:

Let
\mathcal{U}_{i}:=\mathcal{U}[z_{i}]
,
i=1,\cdots,N
and
\sigma=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}
be the linear combination of hyperbolic bubbles that is closest to
u
in
\|\cdot\|_{\lambda}
, i.e.,

\|u-\sigma\|_{\lambda}=\min_{\tilde{\alpha}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{\alpha}_{N}\in\mathbb{R},\\
\tilde{z}_{1},\cdots,\tilde{z}_{N}\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(u-\tilde{\sigma})|^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|u-\tilde{\sigma}|^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}},

and
\tilde{\sigma}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{\alpha}_{i}\mathcal{U}[\tilde{z}_{i}].

Let
\rho:=u-\sigma
. From the hypothesis, it directly follows that
\|\rho\|_{\lambda}\leq\delta
. Furthermore, since the bubbles
\tilde{\mathcal{U}}_{i}
are
\delta-
interacting, the family
(\alpha_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}
is
\delta^{\prime}-
interacting for some
\delta^{\prime}
that goes to
0
as
\delta
goes to
0
.

Summing up, we can say that
qualitatively

\sigma
is a sum of weakly-interacting hyperbolic bubbles and
\|\rho\|_{\lambda}
is small.

Since
\sigma
minimizes
H^{1}_{\lambda}
distance from
u
,
\rho
is
H^{1}_{\lambda}
orthogonal to the manifold composed of linear combination of
N
hyperbolic bubbles (see
[
5
, (5.1)]
), namely the following orthogonality conditions hold:

(5.1)

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho,\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\,-\,\lambda\rho\mathcal{U}_{i}\right){\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0

(5.2)

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho,\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\,-\,\lambda\rho V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\right){\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0

for all
i=1,\ldots,N,j=1,\ldots,n,
and where
V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{i})=\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{U}_{i}\circ\tau_{te_{j}}\big|_{t=0}
.

Since
\mathcal{U}_{i}
,
V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{i})
are eigenfunctions for
\frac{-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda}{\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}}
(see
[
5
, Proposition 3.1]
or Appendix) the above orthogonality conditions are equivalent to

(5.3)

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\,\rho\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0,

(5.4)

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\,\rho\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0.

for all
i=1,\ldots,N,j=1,\ldots,n.

Our goal is to show that
\|\rho\|_{\lambda}
is controlled by
\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+|u|^{p-1}u\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
. To achieve that let us start testing
\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+|u|^{p-1}u
by
\rho
, exploiting the orthogonality condition (
5.1
) yields

\displaystyle\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}[\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho,\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(u-\sigma)\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda\rho(u-\sigma)]\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho,\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda\rho u)\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u-\lambda u-u|u|^{p-1})\rho\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|u|^{p-1}\rho\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\|-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u-\lambda u-u|u|^{p-1}\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\|\rho\|_{H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|u|^{p-1}\rho\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

(5.5)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+u|u|^{p-1}\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|u|^{p-1}\rho\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

To control the last term in (
5
), we use the following elementary estimates

(5.6)

\bigg|(a+b)|a+b|^{p-1}-a|a|^{p-1}\bigg|\leq p|a|^{p-1}|b|+C_{p}\bigg(|a|^{p-2}|b|^{2}+|b|^{p}\bigg),

(5.7)

\bigg|\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\bigg)\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}\bigg|^{p-1}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}|a_{i}|^{p-1}\bigg|\lesssim\sum_{1\leq i\neq k\leq N}|a_{i}|^{p-1}|a_{k}|,

that holds for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
and for any
a_{1},\cdots,a_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
. Applying (
5.6
) with
a=\sigma
and
b=\rho
, and (
5.7
) with
a_{i}=\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}
yields

\bigg|u|u|^{p-1}-|\sigma|^{p-1}\sigma\bigg|\leq p|\sigma|^{p-1}|\rho|+C(|\sigma|^{p-2}|\rho|^{2}+|\rho|^{p})

where
C=C_{n,\lambda,N,p}
and

\bigg|\sigma|\sigma|^{p-1}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}|\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}|^{p-1}\bigg|\lesssim\sum_{1\leq i\neq k\leq N}|\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}|^{p-1}|\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}|.

Combining the above two estimates we deduce

\bigg|u|u|^{p-1}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}|\alpha_{i}|^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\bigg|\leq p|\sigma|^{p-1}|\rho|+C\bigg(|\sigma|^{p-2}|\rho|^{2}+|\rho|^{p}+\sum_{1\leq i\neq k\leq N}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}\bigg).

Therefore, using (
5.3
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|u|^{p-1}\rho\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq p\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\sigma^{p-1}\rho^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\,+\,C\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\sigma|^{p-2}|\rho|^{3}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

(5.8)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\rho|^{p+1}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\sum_{1\leq i\neq k\leq N}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\rho|\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg).

Using Proposition
7.1
from the forthcoming section, we have

p\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\sigma^{p-1}\rho^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\tilde{c}\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2},\quad\mbox{with }\,\tilde{c}=\tilde{c}(n,\lambda,N,p)<1.

Using Hölder and Poincaré-Sobolev inequality we estimate the other terms on the RHS of (
5
) as follows

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\sigma|^{p-2}|\rho|^{3}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\|\rho\|_{L^{p+1}}^{3}\|\sigma\|_{L^{p+1}}^{p-2}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{3},

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\rho|^{p+1}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{p+1},

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\rho|\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\|\rho\|_{L^{p+1}}\|\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}\|_{L^{(p+1)^{\prime}}}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{\lambda}\|\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}\|_{L^{(p+1)^{\prime}}}.

Substituting these estimates into (
5
) yields

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|u|^{p-1}\rho\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{c}(n,\lambda,N)\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}

(5.9)

\displaystyle\qquad+C\bigg(\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{3}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{p+1}+\sum_{1\leq i\neq j\leq N}\|\rho\|_{\lambda}\|\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}\|_{L^{(p+1)^{\prime}}}\bigg).

Next, we control
\|\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}\|_{L^{(p+1)^{\prime}}}
for
i\neq k
. Since
p>2
implies
\min\{(p-1)(p+1)^{\prime},(p+1)^{\prime}\}=(p+1)^{\prime}
. Therefore, by Lemma
4.1
, we find

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}\|_{L^{(p+1)^{\prime}}}=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{(p-1)(p+1)^{\prime}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{(p+1)^{\prime}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{(p+1)^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\approx\big(e^{-c(n,\lambda)(p+1)^{\prime}d(z_{i},z_{k})}\big)^{\frac{1}{(p+1)^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z_{i},z_{k})}

(5.10)

\displaystyle\approx\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{k}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

Moreover, using Lemma
8.1
we get

(5.11)

\max_{i\neq k}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{k}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim\|(\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\lambda)u+|u|^{p-1}u\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}.

Hence, combining (
5
), (
5
) and (
5.11
), we find

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u|u|^{p-1}\rho\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\bigg(\tilde{c}(n,\lambda,N,p)+\varepsilon N^{2}C_{n,\lambda,N,p}\tilde{C}\bigg)\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}+C^{\prime}_{n,\lambda,N,p}\bigg(\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{3}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{p+1}

(5.12)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+u|u|^{p-1}\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\|\rho\|_{\lambda}\bigg),

where
\tilde{C}
is the constant hidden in (
5.11
). We choose
\varepsilon>0
small such that

\tilde{c}(n,\lambda,N,p)+\varepsilon N^{2}C_{n,\lambda,N,p}\tilde{C}<1
. Hence, substituting (
5
) in (
5
) yields

\displaystyle\bigg(1-\tilde{c}(n,\lambda,N,p)-\varepsilon N^{2}C_{n,\lambda,N,p}\tilde{C}\bigg)\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{3}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{p+1}

\displaystyle\qquad+\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+u|u|^{p-1}\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\|\rho\|_{\lambda}.

Since we can assume
\|\rho\|_{\lambda}<<1
, the last inequality implies

(5.13)

\|\rho\|_{\lambda}\lesssim\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+u|u|^{p-1}\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

So, now we are just left to show that the value of all the
\alpha_{i}
can be replaced by
1
; note that thanks to (
5.13
), this fact follows from
Lemma
8.1
. More precisely,
|\alpha_{i}-1|\lesssim\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+u|u|^{p-1}\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
, so it suffices to consider
\sigma^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{U}_{i}
to get that
\sigma^{\prime}
satisfies all the desired conditions.

\square

Proof of Corollary
1.1

Proof.

First assume
p<2^{\star}-1.
Then the result immediately follows from the profile decomposition Theorem
C
, Remark
1.2
and Theorem
1.1
.

Now assume
p=2^{\star}-1,
and let
\lambda\not\in\Lambda.
We know that that
S_{\lambda}
is not a rational multiple of
S
and hence there exists
\delta=\delta(n,N,\lambda)>0
such that the interval
((N-\delta)S_{\lambda},(N+\delta)S_{\lambda})
does not contain an integer multiple of
S.

Should the statement of the corollary be false there must exist a sequence
\{u_{m}\}
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
such that
u_{m}\geq 0,I_{\lambda}(u_{m})\rightarrow\frac{N}{n}S_{\lambda}^{\frac{n}{2}}
and
\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u_{m}+\lambda u_{m}+u_{m}^{p}\|_{H^{-1}}\lesssim\frac{1}{m}\rightarrow 0

as
m\rightarrow\infty.
Then by Theorem
3.1
and Theorem
1.1
the desired bound follows.
∎

6.
localization of family of bubbles

In this section, we collect some prerequisites to prove the improved spectral inequality stated in the next section.

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
n\geq 1
. Given a point
x_{0}\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
and radii
0<r<R
, there exists a Lipschitz bump function
\varphi=\varphi_{r,R}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to[0,1]
such that

\varphi=1
on the geodesic ball
B(x_{0},r)
,
\varphi=0
in
B(x_{0},R)^{\complement}
and

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\varphi(x)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim\frac{1}{R-r}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
x_{0}=0
. As before let
\rho(x)=d(x,0)
denote the geodesic distance of
x
to
0
. Define

\displaystyle\varphi(x)=\begin{cases}1,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mbox{ if }\ \ 0<\rho(x)<r,\\
\sin\bigg[\frac{\pi}{2}\big(\frac{\rho(x)+R-2r}{R-r}\big)\bigg]\ \ \ \mbox{if }\ \ r<\rho(x)<R,\\
0,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\mbox{ if }\ \ \rho(x)\geq R.\\
\end{cases}

Since
\varphi
is radial, by abuse of notation we can write
\varphi(x)=\varphi(\rho)
. Therefore, it only remains to show that

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\varphi(x)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\sup_{\rho}|\varphi^{\prime}(\rho)|\lesssim\frac{1}{R-r},

where
′
denotes the derivative w.r.t.
\rho
. Clearly, for
r<\rho(x)<R

|\varphi^{\prime}(\rho)|=\frac{\pi}{2(R-r)}\bigg|\cos\bigg[\frac{\pi}{2}\big(\frac{\rho(x)+R-2r}{R-r}\big)\bigg]\bigg|\lesssim\frac{1}{R-r},

and
\varphi^{\prime}(\rho)=0
elsewhere. This completes the proof.

∎

As a corollary of Lemma
6.1
, choosing
R
large enough and
r=R/2
, it follows

|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\varphi(x)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=o(1)\quad\mbox{as}\quad R\to\infty.

Lemma 6.2
.

Assume
3\leq n\leq 5
,
p\in(2,2^{\star}-1]
and
\lambda
satisfies
(H1)
. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta=\delta(n,N,\lambda,p,\varepsilon)>0
such that if
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}[z_{i}]
,
i=1,\cdots,N
are
\delta-
interacting hyperbolic bubbles, then for any
1\leq i\leq N
, there exists a Lipschitz bump functions
\varphi_{i}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to[0,1]
such that the followings hold:

(i) Almost all mass of
\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p+1}
is in the region
\{\varphi_{i}=1\}
, i.e.,

\int_{\{\varphi_{i}=1\}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p+1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\geq(1-\varepsilon)S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}.

(ii) In the region
\{\varphi_{i}>0\}
, there holds

\varepsilon\mathcal{U}_{i}>\mathcal{U}_{k}\quad\mbox{for all }\,k\neq i.

(iii) The
L^{\infty}
norm of
|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\varphi_{i}|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
is small, i.e.,

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\ |\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\varphi_{i}(x)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\varepsilon.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume
i=N
and applying hyperbolic translation we can also assume
\mathcal{U}_{N}=\mathcal{U}[0]
. Recall that,

\mathcal{U}_{N}(x)\approx e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,0)},\quad\mathcal{U}_{k}(x)\approx e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,z_{k})},\,k\neq N.

Let
R>1
be a large parameter (we will fix it later depending on
\epsilon
).

\int_{B(0,R/2)^{\complement}}\mathcal{U}_{N}^{p+1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim\int_{R/2}^{\infty}e^{-(p+1)c(n,\lambda)\rho}e^{(n-1)\rho}d\rho\leq C\,\frac{e^{-\big((p+1)c(n,\lambda)-(n-1)\big)R/2}}{(p+1)c(n,\lambda)-(n-1)},

as
(p+1)c(n,\lambda)>n-1
(follows from the fact that
c(n,\lambda)>\frac{n-1}{2}
and
p>1
).
We choose
R
such that

C\,\frac{e^{-\big((p+1)c(n,\lambda)-(n-1)\big)R/2}}{(p+1)c(n,\lambda)-(n-1)}\leq\varepsilon\,S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}

i.e.,

R\ \geq\ \frac{2}{(p+1)c(n,\lambda)-(n-1)}\ln\left({\frac{C}{\big((p+1)c(n,\lambda)-(n-1)\big)\varepsilon S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}}}\right).

Let
\varphi
be the Lipchitz bump function constructed in Lemma
6.1
with
r=R/2
, i.e.,
\varphi=1
in
B(0,R/2)
,
\varphi=0
in
B(0,R)^{\complement}
and

|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\varphi|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim 2/R
.
We further enlarge
R
(in addition to the previous choice), if required, to ensure that
2/R<\varepsilon
. Then,

\int_{\{\varphi=1\}}\mathcal{U}_{N}^{p+1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{N}^{p+1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\int_{B(0,R/2)^{\complement}}\mathcal{U}_{N}^{p+1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\geq S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}-\varepsilon S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}=(1-\varepsilon)S_{\lambda,p}^{\frac{p+1}{p-1}}.

Moreover,
\sup_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\varphi(x)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim 2/R<\varepsilon
. Hence (i) and (iii) hold.

Now we will determine
\delta
. Note that
\{\varphi>0\}=B(0,R)
. If
x\in B(0,R)
, using the decay estimate of
\mathcal{U}_{N}
and
\mathcal{U}_{k}
,
k\neq N
, it follows

\mathcal{U}_{N}(x)\gtrsim e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,0)}\gtrsim e^{-c(n,\lambda)R}

\mathcal{U}_{k}(x)\lesssim e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,z_{k})}\lesssim e^{c(n,\lambda)R}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(0,z_{k})},

as
d(x,z_{k})\geq d(0,z_{k})-d(x,0)
. To hold
\varepsilon\mathcal{U}_{N}>\mathcal{U}_{k}
,
k\neq N
in
B(0,R)
, we need to show that

\varepsilon e^{-c(n,\lambda)R}\geq C_{1}e^{c(n,\lambda)R}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(0,z_{k})},

for some constant
C_{1}>0
. In other words, we need
d(0,z_{k})\geq\frac{1}{c(n,\lambda)}\ln\big(\frac{C_{1}e^{2c(n,\lambda)R}}{\varepsilon}\big)
. Since
z_{i}
’s are
\delta-
interacting bubbles, from the definition we already have

\max_{i\neq k}e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(z_{i},z_{k})}\leq\delta,

i.e.,
d(z_{i},z_{k})\geq\frac{\ln\delta^{-1}}{c(n,\lambda)}
for all
k\neq i
. So we choose
\delta>0
such that

\ln\delta^{-1}>\ln\Big(\frac{C_{1}e^{2c(n,\lambda)R}}{\varepsilon}\Big),

then clearly
\mathcal{U}_{i}
s are
\delta-
interacting bubbles imply,

\varepsilon\mathcal{U}_{N}>\mathcal{U}_{k},\quad k\neq N,

on the set
\{\varphi>0\}
. Note that
\delta\lesssim\epsilon.
Taking
\varphi_{N}=\varphi
completes the proof.
∎

7.
Improved spectral inequality

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
3\leq n\leq 5
,
p\in(2,2^{\star}-1]
and
\lambda
satisfies
(H1)
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
. There exists a positive constant
\delta=\delta(n,N,\lambda,p)
such that if
\sigma=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}[z_{i}]
is a linear combination of
\delta-
interacting hyperbolic bubbles and
\rho\in H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
satisfy the orthogonality conditions

(7.1)

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho\,\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0\quad\mbox{and }\,\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho\,\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0\quad\mbox{for all }\,1\leq i\leq N,\,\,1\leq j\leq n,

where
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}[z_{i}]
.
Then

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\sigma^{p-1}\rho^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\frac{\tilde{c}}{p}\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2},

where
\tilde{c}=\tilde{c}(n,N,\lambda,p)
is a positive constant strictly less than 1.

Proof.

Fix a parameter
\varepsilon>0
and in this proof, we will denote with
o(1)
any quantity that goes to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. By Lemma
6.2
, there exists
\delta
,
\varphi_{i}
satisfying (i), (ii) and (iii) of Lemma
6.2
.
Note that (i) implies the sets
\{\varphi_{i}=1\}
,
i=1,\cdots,N
are disjoint as almost all the masses of
\mathcal{U}_{i}
is concentrated on
\{\varphi_{i}=1\}
. Moreover, (ii) implies
\varphi_{i}
’s have disjoint support. Therefore,

\mathbb{B}^{n}\subset\cup_{i=1}^{N}\Big\{\varphi_{i}=1\Big\}\cup\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varphi_{i}<1\Big\}.

By Lemma
6.2
(ii) and
\delta
-interaction of
\mathcal{U}_{i}
, on the set
\{\varphi_{i}>0\}
,

\sigma^{p-1}=(\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}+\sum_{k\neq i}\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k})^{p-1}\leq(1+CN\varepsilon)^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}=(1+o(1))\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\sigma^{p-1}\rho^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\{\varphi_{i}=1\}}\sigma^{p-1}\rho^{2}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\int_{\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varphi_{i}<1\}}\ \sigma^{p-1}\rho^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+o(1))\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\{\varphi_{i}=1\}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\rho^{2}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\bigg(\int_{\{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varphi_{i}<1\}}\sigma^{p+1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{p-1}{p+1}}\|\rho\|^{2}_{L^{p+1}}

\displaystyle\leq(1+o(1))\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\rho\varphi_{i})^{2}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\{\varphi_{i}<1\}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p+1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{p-1}{p+1}}\|\rho\|^{2}_{\lambda}

(7.2)

\displaystyle\leq(1+o(1))\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\rho\varphi_{i})^{2}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\varepsilon^{\frac{p-1}{p+1}}\|\rho\|^{2}_{\lambda}

Claim:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\rho\varphi_{i})^{2}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\frac{1}{\Lambda}\|(\rho\varphi_{i})\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(1)\|\rho\|^{2}_{\lambda}\quad\mbox{for }\,i=1,\cdots,N
,

where
\Lambda
is the smallest eigenvalue of

\frac{-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda}{\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}}
, which is
bigger than
p
.

To prove the claim, let
\psi
be either
\mathcal{U}_{i}
or
V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{i})
,
1\leq j\leq n
normalized so that

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\psi^{2}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=1
. Hence, thanks to the orthogonality condition (
7.1
), it holds that

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho\,\varphi_{i}\,\psi\,\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho\,\psi\,\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}(1-\varphi_{i})\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg|\leq\bigg|\int_{\{\varphi_{i}<1\}}\rho\,\psi\,\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\rho|^{p+1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{p+1}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\psi^{2}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\int_{\{\varphi_{i}<1\}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p+1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{p-1}{2(p+1)}}

\displaystyle\leq o(1)\|\rho\|_{\lambda},

where in the last inequality we have applied Lemma
6.2
(i).

This proves that
\rho\varphi_{i}
is almost orthogonal to
\psi
. Hence, using
[
5
, Proposition 3.1]
, it follows that if
\Lambda
is the smallest eigenvalue of

(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda)/\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}

bigger than
p
then

(7.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\rho\varphi_{i})^{2}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\frac{1}{\Lambda}\|(\rho\varphi_{i})\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(1)\|\rho\|^{2}_{\lambda}.

This proves the claim.

We now estimate RHS of (
7.3
).

(7.4)

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\rho\varphi_{i})|^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho|^{2}\varphi_{i}^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho^{2}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\varphi_{i}|^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+2\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho\varphi_{i}\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\varphi_{i}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

Using Lemma
6.2
(iii) and Poincaré inequality,

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho^{2}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\varphi_{i}|^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\varepsilon^{2}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=o(1)\|\rho\|^{2}_{\lambda}.

Next,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho\varphi_{i}\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho\cdot\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\varphi_{i}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho^{2}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\varphi_{i}|^{2}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho|^{2}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}\|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim o(1)\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}.

Inserting the above two estimates into (
7.4
), we find

\|(\rho\varphi_{i})\|_{\lambda}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg(|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\rho\varphi_{i})|^{2}-\lambda(\rho\varphi_{i})^{2}\bigg)\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\bigg(|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho|^{2}-\lambda\rho^{2}\bigg)\varphi_{i}^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+o(1)\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}.

Taking a sum of
i=1
to
N
and using the fact that
\varphi_{i}
s have disjoint support, it follows

(7.5)

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\|(\rho\varphi_{i})\|_{\lambda}^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq(1+o(1))\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}.

Thus combining (
7
), (
7.3
) and (
7.5
), we deduce

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\sigma^{p-1}\rho^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\leq\frac{1+o(1)}{\Lambda}\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}\leq\frac{\tilde{c}}{p}\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2},

with
\tilde{c}<1
(since
\Lambda>p
).
∎

8.
Interaction integral estimates

In this section, we prove the following interaction integral estimate:

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
3\leq n\leq 5,\ N\in\mathbb{N},p\in(2,2^{\star}-1]
and
\lambda
be as in
(H1)
. For every
\epsilon>0,
there exists a
\delta>0

depending on
n,p,N,\lambda,\epsilon
such that if
(\alpha_{i},\mathcal{U}[z_{i}])_{1\leq i\leq N}
is a family of
\delta
-interacting hyperbolic bubbles satisfying the orthogonality conditions (
5.3
) and (
5.4
) then setting
u=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}[z_{i}]+\rho
the following estimates hold:

\displaystyle\max_{i\neq k}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim\|(\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\lambda)u+|u|^{p-1}u\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2},

and

\displaystyle\max_{i}|\alpha_{i}-1|\lesssim\|(\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\lambda)u+|u|^{p-1}u\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}.

8.1.
A geometric lemma

To prove the above lemma we take the help of the following geometrically intuitive lemma.

Lemma 8.2
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}[z_{i}],\ 1\leq i\leq N
be a family of
\delta
-interacting hyperbolic bubbles. Then there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that for every
\delta<\delta_{0},
there exists an indices
k\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and a direction
e_{j}
such that after applications of hyperbolic translations and rotations, the followings hold:

(i)

z_{k}=0,

(ii)

all
z_{i},i\neq k
lie in the negative half
\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}:=\{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\ |\ x_{j}<0\}.

(iii)

There exists a constant
\kappa=\kappa(\delta)>0
such that
(z_{i})_{j}
(the
j
-th component if
z_{i}
) satisfies

(z_{i})_{j}\leq-\kappa
for all
i\neq k.

Proof.

Since there are only
N
-many indices we can find two indices
k_{1},k_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
such that

\displaystyle d(z_{k_{1}},z_{k_{2}})=\max_{i\neq k}\ d(z_{i},z_{k}).

There may be other choices of such indices, but we only need a pair of them.
By rearranging the indices and by applying a hyperbolic translation we may assume
z_{1}=0
and
d(z_{1},z_{N})=\max_{i\neq k}d(z_{i},z_{k}).
Further applying an orthogonal transformation we may assume
z_{N}=|z_{N}|e_{j}.

Recall that the hyperbolic distances are preserved under the orthogonal group of transformations.

We will show that if
\mathcal{U}[z_{i}]
are sufficiently small
\delta
-interacting then
\tau_{-z_{N}}(z_{i}),1\leq i\leq N
satisfies all the properties of the stated lemma.

For the simplicity of notations we denote
z_{N}=z.
Recall that
\tau_{-z}=\sigma_{-z^{*}}\rho_{-z^{*}}
is the composition of reflection
\rho_{-z*}
with respect to the plane
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\ |\ x\cdot z^{*}=0\}
and the inversion with respect to the sphere

S(-z^{*},r)
where
r
is determined by
r^{2}=|z^{*}|^{2}-1=\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}-1.
We divide
\mathbb{B}^{n}
into the regions
I_{1},I_{2}
and
I_{3}
defined below (see Figure
3
):

I_{2}
I_{2}
z*
-z*
z
I_{1}
-z
I_{3}

Figure 3
.
The above picture demonstrating the regions
I_{1},I_{2}
and
I_{3}.
The thick boundary portion of
B_{E}(z^{*},r)
in
\mathbb{B}^{n}
is included in
I_{1},
and similarly for
I_{3}.

I_{2}
is the open region in
\mathbb{B}^{n}
complementing
I_{1}\cup I_{2}
. All the
z_{i}
s which lie outside
I_{1}
will map under the hyperbolic translation to the region
I_{3}.
The boundary portion of
I_{1}
will map to the boundary portion of
I_{3}.

\displaystyle I_{1}=\mathbb{B}^{n}\cap\overline{B_{E}(z^{*},r)}\ \ \ I_{3}=\mathbb{B}^{n}\cap\overline{B_{E}(-z^{*},r)}\ \ \ \mbox{and}\ \ \ I_{2}=\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash(I_{1}\cup I_{3}).

The following observations are easy to verify:

(a)

\sigma_{-z^{*}}
leaves
S(-z^{*},r)
invariant.

(b)

z\in B_{E}(z^{*},r)
as direct computation shows
|z-z^{*}|^{2}=r^{2}(1-|z|^{2})<r^{2}.

(c)

\rho_{-z^{*}}(I_{1})=I_{3},\rho_{-z^{*}}(I_{3})=I_{1}
and
\rho_{-z^{*}}
leaves
I_{2}
invariant.

(d)

\sigma_{-z^{*}}(I_{1}\cup I_{2})\subset I_{3}
and
\sigma_{-z^{*}}(I_{3}\backslash S(-z^{*},r))\subset I_{1}\cup I_{2}.

(e)

\sigma_{-z^{*}}
sends spheres
S(-z^{*},r_{1})
to spheres
S(-z^{*},\frac{r^{2}}{r_{1}}).

According to our choice all
z_{i}
’s lie with in
\overline{B_{E}(0,|z|)}.

The above observation indicates all points
z_{i}
such that
z_{i}\not\in\overline{B_{E}(0,|z|)}\cap B_{E}(z^{*},r)
would translate
under the hyperbolic translation
\tau_{-z}
to the region
I_{3}.
Clearly
\tau_{-z}(z)=0.
Now let
z_{i}\in\overline{B_{E}(0,|z|)}\cap B_{E}(z^{*},r).
Then
\rho_{-z^{*}}(z_{i})\in B_{E}(-z^{*},r)\cap\overline{B_{E}(0,|z|)}.
Let us now look at the action of
\sigma_{-z^{*}}
on the set
B_{E}(-z^{*},r)\cap\overline{B_{E}(0,|z|)}.

According to
(e),

\sigma_{-z^{*}}
maps
B_{E}(-z^{*},r)\cap\overline{B_{E}(0,|z|)}
to the region
\overline{B_{E}(-z^{*},\frac{1}{|z|})}.
To see that we let
r_{0}=|z-z^{*}|=|z|r^{2}.
Then by
(e),

\sigma_{-z^{*}}
maps
S(-z^{*},r_{0})
to
S(-z^{*},\frac{1}{|z|})
and leaves
S(-z^{*},r)
invariant. Since all the
\rho_{-z^{*}}(z_{i})
s are trapped with in the spheres
S(-z^{*},r)
and

S(-z^{*},r_{0})
we conclude
\tau_{-z}(z_{i}),i\neq N
would lie with in
\overline{B(-z^{*},\frac{1}{|z|})}\backslash\{0\}
(see Figure
4
). This proves
(i)
and
(ii)
.

0
\mathcal{R}_{0}=B_{E}(0,|z|)\cap B_{E}(z^{*},r)
z*
x
distance at least
\kappa
-z*
-e_{j}
e_{j}

Figure 4
.
Under the action of hyperbolic translation
\tau_{-z},

z
will map to
0
and all other
z_{i}
s which lie in the region
\mathcal{R}_{0}
will map to the largest ball centered at
-z^{*}
and radius
\frac{1}{|z|}.
After the translation if
x
denotes the point
\tau_{-z}(z_{i})
then
x
is at least
\kappa
distance apart from the plane
\{x_{j}=0\}
.

Now we claim that if
\mathcal{U}[z_{i}],1\leq i\leq N
are sufficiently small
\delta
-interacting then
(iii)
holds (cf. Figure
4
).
Fix
i\neq N
, for simplicity we denote
x=\tau_{-z}(z_{i})
and we write
x=(x^{\prime},x_{j})
to indicate the
j
-th component. The above discussions yields
x\in\overline{B(-z^{*},\frac{1}{|z|})}\backslash\{0\}
i.e,

(8.1)

\displaystyle\left|x_{j}+\frac{z_{j}}{|z|^{2}}\right|^{2}+|x^{\prime}|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}.

If
\delta
is small then
d(z_{i},z_{N})=d(x,0)=\ln\left(\frac{1+|x|}{1-|x|}\right)
is sufficiently large. That is there exists small
\eta=\eta(\delta)>0
such that
|x|>1-\eta.
Hence from (
8.1
) denoting
t=\frac{1}{|z|^{2}}
we deduce

\displaystyle(x_{j}+tz_{j})^{2}+|x^{\prime}|^{2}=|x|^{2}+2tx_{j}z_{j}+t^{2}z_{j}^{2}\leq t

\displaystyle i.e.\

\displaystyle x_{j}\leq\frac{t-t^{2}z_{j}^{2}-|x|^{2}}{2tz_{j}}\leq-\frac{(1-\eta)^{3}}{2}=:-\kappa,

where we have used
z_{j}=|z|>1-\eta
as
d(0,z)=d(z_{1},z_{N})>>1.
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.1
.

The point
(ii)
is redundant given point
(iii)
of the above lemma. We wanted to emphasise that
(i)
and
(ii)
are the properties of the hyperbolic translation while
(iii)
is the property of the
\delta
-interaction.

We are now in a position to prove the interaction integral estimates Lemma
8.1
.

8.2.
The proof of interaction integral estimates

Given
u
and
(\alpha_{i},\mathcal{U}[z_{i}])_{i=1}^{N}
a family of
\delta
-interacting hyperbolic bubbles as in Lemma
8.1
. We put

(8.2)

\displaystyle u=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}+\rho:=\sigma+\rho,

where
\mathcal{U}_{i}=\mathcal{U}[z_{i}]
and
\sigma=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}.
Recall that the following orthogonality conditions hold

(8.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\lambda)\mathcal{U}_{i}\rho\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\rho\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0

(8.4)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\lambda)V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\rho\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=p\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{i})\rho\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0,\ \ \mbox{for}\ 1\leq j\leq n,

for all
i=1,\ldots,N.
We compute

(8.5)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+|u|^{p-1}u

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho+\lambda\rho-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}+|\sigma+\rho|^{p-1}(\sigma+\rho).

Putting
f=-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u-\lambda u-|u|^{p-1}u
we can rewrite the equation (
8.5
) as

(8.6)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho+\lambda\rho+p\sigma^{p-1}\rho+I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+f=0,

where

(8.7)

\displaystyle I_{1}=\sigma^{p}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p},\ \ I_{2}=|\sigma+\rho|^{p-1}(\sigma+\rho)-\sigma^{p}-p\sigma^{p-1}\rho,\ \ I_{3}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\alpha_{i}^{p}-\alpha_{i})\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}

We follow the approach of
[
19
]
and divide the proof of Lemma
8.1
into several small lemmas. We point out that the next four lemmas hold in any dimension
n\geq 3.

Lemma 8.3
.

We have

(8.8)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}I_{1}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\sum_{i\neq k}\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\alpha_{i}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+o(Q).

for all
1\leq k\leq N
and
j=1,\ldots,n.

Proof.

We denote
\sigma_{k}=\sum_{i\neq k}\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}
so that
\sigma=\sigma_{k}+\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}
holds. We decompose the integral
\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}I_{1}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
into the following four integrals:

\displaystyle J_{1}:=\int_{\{N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\geq\sigma_{k}\}}\left(p\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p-1}\sigma_{k}-\sum_{i\neq k}\alpha_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\right)V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle J_{2}=\int_{\{N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\geq\sigma_{k}\}}\left(\sigma^{p}-\alpha_{k}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p}-p\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p-1}\sigma_{k}\right)V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle J_{3}=\int_{\{\sigma_{k}>N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\left(p\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\sigma_{k}^{p-1}+\sigma_{k}^{p}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\alpha_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\right)V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle J_{4}=\int_{\{\sigma_{k}>N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\left(\sigma^{p}-\sigma_{k}^{p}-p\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\sigma_{k}^{p-1}\right)V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

We now estimate each integral one by one. Using
|V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})|\lesssim\mathcal{U}_{k}
and using the inequality
|(a+b)^{p}-a^{p}-pa^{p-1}b|\lesssim a^{p-2}b^{2}
whenever
0\leq b\lesssim a
we estimate

\displaystyle|J_{2}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\{N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\geq\sigma_{k}\}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p-2}\sigma_{k}^{2}\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\{N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\geq\sigma_{k}\}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p-\epsilon}\sigma_{k}^{1+\epsilon}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

(8.9)

\displaystyle\lesssim Q_{k}^{1+\epsilon}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle|J_{4}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\{\sigma_{k}>N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\sigma_{k}^{p-2}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{2}\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\{\sigma_{k}>N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\sigma_{k}^{p-\epsilon}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{1+\epsilon}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

(8.10)

\displaystyle\lesssim Q_{k}^{1+\epsilon}.

For
J_{3}
we approach as follows.

\displaystyle|J_{3}|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\{\sigma_{k}>N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\sigma_{k}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{2}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\int_{\{\sigma_{k}>N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\Big|\sigma_{k}^{p}-\sum_{i\neq k}\alpha_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\Big|\ \mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

(8.11)

\displaystyle\ +\int_{\{\sigma_{k}>N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p+1}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

The first term in (
8.2
) can be estimated as in
(J2),(J4)
to obtain
O(Q_{k}^{1+\epsilon}).

For the second term we use the inequality
|(\sum_{i}a_{i})^{p}-\sum_{i}a_{i}^{p}|\lesssim\sum_{i\neq j}|a_{i}|^{p-1}\min\{|a_{i}|,|a_{j}|\}
to get

\displaystyle\int_{\{\sigma_{k}>N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\Big|\sigma_{k}^{p}-\sum_{i\neq k}\alpha_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\Big|\ \mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i<j,i\neq k,j\neq k}\int_{\{\sigma_{k}>N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{j}\mathcal{U}_{k}

\displaystyle\lesssim Q^{1+\epsilon},

where in the last line we used Lemma
4.2
.
For the last term in (
8.2
) we see that
\{\sigma_{k}>N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}\subset\cup_{i\neq k}\{\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}>\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}.
Hence

\displaystyle\int_{\{\sigma_{k}>N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p+1}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\neq k}\int_{\{\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}>\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p+1}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\{\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}>\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p-\epsilon}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{1+\epsilon}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim Q^{1+\epsilon}.

Now for the estimation of
J_{1}
, we see that

\displaystyle J_{1}-\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}p\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p-1}\sigma_{k}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

(8.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\{N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}<\sigma_{k}\}}p\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p-1}\sigma_{k}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\sum_{i\neq k}\int_{\{N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\geq\sigma_{k}\}}\alpha_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=J_{1}^{1}+J_{2}^{2}.

J_{1}^{1},J_{2}^{2}
can be estimated in a similar way

\displaystyle\Big|J_{1}^{1}\Big|\lesssim\int_{\{N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}<\sigma_{k}\}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p}\sigma_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim\int_{\{N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}<\sigma_{k}\}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p-\epsilon}\sigma_{k}^{1+\epsilon}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim Q^{1+\epsilon},

and since on
\{\sigma_{k}\leq N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\},

\mathcal{U}_{i}\lesssim\mathcal{U}_{k}
holds for all
i\neq k

\displaystyle\Big|J_{1}^{2}\Big|\lesssim\sum_{i\neq k}\int_{\{\sigma_{k}\leq N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim\sum_{i\neq k}\int_{\{\sigma_{k}\leq N\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}\}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-\epsilon}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{1+\epsilon}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim Q^{1+\epsilon}.

Combining all the estimates and integrating by parts gives the result as indicated below

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}I_{1}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\sum_{l=1}^{4}J_{l}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}p\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p-1}\sigma_{k}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+o(Q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq k}\alpha_{i}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}p\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{i}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+o(Q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq k}\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\alpha_{i}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda)V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+o(Q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq k}\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\alpha_{i}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda)\mathcal{U}_{i}\ V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+o(Q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\neq k}\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\alpha_{i}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\ V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+o(Q).

This completes the proof.

∎

Lemma 8.4
.

Denoting
\xi=
either
\mathcal{U}_{k}
or
V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})
for
1\leq k\leq N,
the following estimate holds.

(8.13)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}I_{2}\xi\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Big|\lesssim\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{\min\{p,2\}}.

Proof.

We again use the elementary inequality
|(a+b)|a+b|^{p-1}-a^{p}-p\sigma^{p-1}b|\leq|b|^{p}+|a|^{p-2}|b|^{2}
and
|\xi|\lesssim\mathcal{U}_{k}

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}I_{2}\xi\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\rho|^{p}\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\sigma^{p-2}|\rho|^{2}\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{L^{p+1}}^{p}\|\mathcal{U}_{k}\|_{L^{p+1}}+\|\sigma\|_{L^{p+1}}^{p-2}\|\rho\|_{L^{p+1}}^{2}\|\mathcal{U}_{k}\|_{L^{p+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{p}+\|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{p}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{\min\{p,2\}}\quad\text{(as}\quad\|\rho\|_{\lambda}\mbox{ is small)}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 8.5
.

We have

(8.14)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}I_{3}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\sum_{i\neq k}(\alpha_{i}^{p}-\alpha_{i})\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

for all
1\leq k\leq N
and
j=1,\ldots,n.

Proof.

We note that

\displaystyle(p+1)\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=(p+1)\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p}\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}(\mathcal{U}_{k}\circ\tau_{te_{j}})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(\mathcal{U}_{k}\circ\tau_{te_{j}})^{p+1}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0.

Hence only the terms
i\neq k
will survive

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}I_{3}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\alpha_{i}^{p}-\alpha_{i})\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\sum_{i\neq k}(\alpha_{i}^{p}-\alpha_{i})\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

∎

Lemma 8.6
.

Given
\epsilon>0
there exists a
\delta>0
depending on
n,N,p,\lambda,\epsilon
such that if
(\alpha_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i})_{i=1}^{N}
is a family of
\delta
-interacting hyperbolic bubbles satisfying the orthogonality conditions (
8.3
),(
8.4
) then

(8.15)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}p\sigma^{p-1}\rho\xi\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Big|\lesssim(Q_{k}^{\min\{p-1,1\}}+\epsilon)\|\rho\|_{\lambda},

where
\xi=
either
\mathcal{U}_{k}
of
V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k}),
for every
1\leq k\leq N,j=1,\ldots,n,
and
Q_{k}
is defined by
Q_{k}=\max_{i\neq k}Q_{ik}.

Proof.

Given
\epsilon>0,
we use the following elementary inequality

|(a+b)^{p-1}-a^{p-1}|\leq C(\epsilon)b^{p-1}+\epsilon a^{p-1}
for every
a,b>0,p>1.
Using the orthogonality conditions and applying the above elementary inequality with
a=\alpha_{k}\mathcal{U}_{k}
and
b=\sum_{i\neq k}\alpha_{i}\mathcal{U}_{i}
so that
a+b=\sigma
we get

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}p\sigma^{p-1}\rho\xi\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}p(\sigma^{p-1}-\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p-1})\rho\xi\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i\neq k}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}|\rho|\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\epsilon\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p}|\rho|\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i\neq k}\|\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{k}\|_{L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}}\|\rho\|_{L^{p+1}}+\epsilon\|\mathcal{U}_{k}\|_{L^{p+1}}^{p}\|\rho\|_{L^{p+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim(Q_{k}^{\min\{p-1,1\}}+\epsilon)\|\rho\|_{\lambda}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.1
.

Proof.

We proceed inductively. First note that in dimension
3\leq n\leq 5
,
2^{\star}-2>1,
and recall our assumption is
2<p\leq 2^{\star}-1,
so that in either case
p-1>1.
Let
k
be the indices and
e_{j}
be the direction as obtained in Lemma
8.2
. We test (
8.6
) against
V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})
and integrate and use the orthogonality conditions to get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}I_{1}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}I_{3}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}p\sigma^{p-1}\rho V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}I_{2}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}fV_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

By Lemma
8.3
-
8.6
and using
\min\{p-1,1\}=1
we get

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq k}(\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i}^{p}-\alpha_{i})\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

(8.16)

\displaystyle\lesssim\ \

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+(Q_{k}+\epsilon)\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(Q).

By Lemma
4.4
we see that
\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\approx Q_{ik}

for all
i\neq k
and thanks to Lemma
8.2
the constant in
\approx
depends only on
n,N,\lambda,p
and
\epsilon.
Moreover, since the family
(\alpha_{i},\mathcal{U}_{i})
are
\delta
-interacting with
\delta

sufficiently small we conclude
(\alpha_{k}^{p-1}\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i}^{p}-\alpha_{i})\approx 1
for all
1\leq i\leq N.
Hence (
8.2
) gives

\displaystyle Q_{k}=\max_{i\neq k}Q_{ik}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+(Q_{k}+\epsilon)\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(Q)

(8.17)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(Q),

where we have used
\delta
is sufficiently small to absorb the term
Q_{k}\|\rho\|_{\lambda}
. Now testing (
8.6
) against
\mathcal{U}_{k}
and using the orthogonality conditions we get

(8.18)

\displaystyle\sum_{i}(\alpha_{i}^{p}-\alpha_{i})\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=-\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(I_{1}+I_{2}+p\sigma^{p-1}\rho+f)\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

It follows from Lemma
8.4
and Lemma
8.6
that if
\delta
is small then

(8.19)

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(I_{2}+p\sigma^{p-1}\rho+f)\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Big|\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}.

On the other hand, the inequality
|(\sum_{i}a_{i})^{p}-\sum_{i}a_{i}^{p}|\lesssim\sum_{i\neq j}a_{i}^{p-1}a_{j}
and using the estimate (
8.2
) we get

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}I_{1}\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i\neq j}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p-1}\mathcal{U}_{j}\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim Q_{k}\,+\,o(Q)

(8.20)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}\,+\,o(Q).

Combining (
8.18
), (
8.19
), (
8.2
) and using (
8.2
) we get

\displaystyle|\alpha_{k}^{p}-\alpha_{k}|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p+1}{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\neq k}|\alpha_{i}^{p}-\alpha_{i}|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}\mathcal{U}_{k}+\Big|\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(I_{1}+I_{2}+p\sigma^{p-1}\rho+f)\mathcal{U}_{k}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Big|

(8.21)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(Q).

This gives the desired estimate on
\alpha_{k}
i.e.

(8.22)

\displaystyle|\alpha_{k}-1|\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(Q).

We proceed inductively as follows. Discard the indices
k
from
\{1,\ldots,N\}
and apply Lemma
8.2
to find indices
l
(
\neq k
) and a direction which we again call
e_{j}.
We proceed as before and get

(8.23)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\neq l}(\alpha_{l}^{p-1}\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i}^{p}-\alpha_{i})\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{l})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(Q).

The terms corresponding to
i=k
in (
8.23
) can be estimated by
Q_{k}
and hence

\displaystyle\max_{i\neq l,i\neq k}Q_{il}\lesssim\sum_{i\neq l,i\neq k}(\alpha_{l}^{p-1}\alpha_{i}+\alpha_{i}^{p}-\alpha_{i})\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{i}^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{k})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\ \

\displaystyle\Big|(\alpha_{l}^{p-1}\alpha_{k}+\alpha_{k}^{p}-\alpha_{k})\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{p}V_{j}(\mathcal{U}_{l})\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Big|+\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(Q)

\displaystyle\lesssim\ \

\displaystyle\ Q_{k}+\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(Q)

\displaystyle\lesssim\ \

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(Q).

Similarly we obtain the estimate
|\alpha_{l}-1|\leq\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(Q).
Proceeding in this way and by deleting the indices one by one we get

\displaystyle Q=\max_{i}Q_{i}\lesssim\|f\|_{H^{-1}}+\epsilon\|\rho\|_{\lambda}+\|\rho\|_{\lambda}^{2}+o(Q).

Since
\delta
is sufficiently small we get the desired estimate on
Q
which in turn gives the desired estimate on

\max_{i}|\alpha_{i}-1|
as derived in (
8.2
)-(
8.22
), completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

9.
Counter examples for
1<p\leq 2
: main theorem and technical results

In this section, we prove that the estimate

\displaystyle\delta(u)\lesssim\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+u^{p}\|_{H^{-1}},

fails whenever
1<p\leq 2
in any dimension
n\geq 3
and
\lambda
satisfying
(H1)
. The main theorem of this section is as follows:

Theorem 9.1
.

Let
n\geq 3
and
1<p\leq 2
such that
\lambda,p
satisfy
(H1)
. There exists a family
u_{R}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
parametrized by
R\in(0,1)
such that the following
statements hold :

(a)

Let
\mathcal{D}:=\inf_{c_{1},c_{2},z_{1},z_{2}}\|u_{R}-c_{1}\,\mathcal{U}[z_{1}]-c_{2}\mathcal{U}[z_{2}]\|_{\lambda},
then for
R
sufficiently close to 1,

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u_{R}+\lambda u_{R}+|u_{R}|^{p-1}u_{R}\|_{L^{(p+1)^{\prime}}}\lesssim\begin{cases}\mathcal{D}|\log\mathcal{D}|^{-\frac{1}{2}},\quad\mbox{if}\ p=2\\
\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\mathcal{D}^{1+\alpha_{0}},\quad\ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ 1<p<2,\end{cases}

where
\alpha_{0}>0
is a constant depending on
n,\lambda
and
p.

(b)

If we let
R\uparrow 1,
then the family
u_{R}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|u_{R}-\mathcal{U}[-Re_{1}]-\mathcal{U}[Re_{1}]\|_{\lambda}\rightarrow 0,

where
\mathcal{U}
is the standard hyperbolic bubbles and
e_{1}=(1,0,\ldots,0).

(c)

Let
\mathcal{D^{+}}:=\inf_{c_{1},c_{2},z_{1},z_{2}}\|u_{R}^{+}-c_{1}\,\mathcal{U}[z_{1}]-c_{2}\mathcal{U}[z_{2}]\|_{\lambda},
where
u^{+}=\mbox{max}\{u,0\},
then

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u_{R}^{+}+\lambda u_{R}+|u_{R}|^{p-1}u_{R}\|_{H^{-1}}\lesssim\begin{cases}\mathcal{D^{+}}|(\log\mathcal{D}^{+})|^{-\frac{1}{4}},\quad\mbox{if}\ p=2\\
\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt(\mathcal{D^{+}})^{1+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}},\quad\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \mbox{if}\ 1<p<2,\end{cases}

where
\alpha_{0}>0
is the positive constant in
(a).
If we let
R\uparrow 1,
then the family
u_{R}^{+}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|u_{R}^{+}-\mathcal{U}[-Re_{1}]-\mathcal{U}[Re_{1}]\|_{\lambda}\rightarrow 0.

Remark 9.1
.

In particular, Theorem
1.1
does not hold when
1<p\leq 2.
Moreover, since
2^{\star}-1=\frac{n+2}{n-2}\leq 2
if
n\geq 6,
therefore Theorem
1.1
does not hold in dimension
n\geq 6.

9.1.
Notations and preliminaries

We know that the eigenvalues of
(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda)/\mathcal{U}[z]^{p-1}
are independent of
z.
The first two eigenvalues are
1
and
p
respectively and the corresponding eigenspace is generated by
(n+1)
vectors

\{\mathcal{U}[z],V_{1}(\mathcal{U}[z]),\ldots,V_{n}(\mathcal{U}[z])\}.
Let
\Theta
be the smallest eigenvalue strictly bigger than
p
. We fix
\epsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)^{2}=\frac{p}{\Theta},\ \ \ \ i.e.\ \ \ \ \ (1-\epsilon)\Theta=\frac{p}{1-\epsilon}.

We denote
U=\mathcal{U}[-Re_{1}],V=\mathcal{U}[Re_{1}],
where
R\in(0,1)
and define

(9.1)

\displaystyle f=(U+V)^{p}-U^{p}-V^{p}.

Note that
d(x,Re_{1})\leq d(x,-Re_{1})
if
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}:=\{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\ :\ x_{1}>0\}
and
d(x,Re_{1})\geq d(x,-Re_{1})
in
\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}:=\{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\ :\ x_{1}<0\}.
Hence
U\geq V
in
\mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}
and
U\leq V
in
\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}.
Since
1<p\leq 2
we deduce

\displaystyle f\approx\begin{cases}UV^{p-1},\ \ \ \ \mbox{in}\ \mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}\\
U^{p-1}V,\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{in}\ \mathbb{B}^{n}_{-}.\end{cases}

\bullet

We denote by
\mathcal{F}
the subspace generated by all the eigenfunctions of

(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda)/U^{p-1}
and
(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda)/V^{p-1}
with eigenvalues less than

(1-\epsilon)\Theta.
That is

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\mbox{span}\ B_{\mathcal{F}},\ \mbox{where}\ B_{\mathcal{F}}=\{U,V,V_{j}(U),V_{j}(V),\ 1\leq j\leq n\}

where we recall
V_{j}(U)=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}U\circ\tau_{te_{j}}
and hence dim
\mathcal{F}=2(n+1).

\bullet

Let
\mathcal{E}
be the subspace generated by all the eigenfunctions of

(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda)/(U+V)^{p-1}
with eigenvalues less than

(1-\epsilon)\Theta.
We denote by
B_{\mathcal{E}}
a basis for
\mathcal{E}.

\bullet

Let
\pi:L^{2}_{(U+V)^{p-1}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathcal{E}^{\bot}\subset L^{2}_{(U+V)^{p-1}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
be the orthogonal projection onto the subspace orthogonal to
\mathcal{E}.
We recall

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\bot}:=\Big\{g\in L^{2}_{(U+V)^{p-1}}\ |\ \int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}g\psi(U+V)^{p-1}\ {\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0,\ \ \mbox{for all}\ \psi\in\mathcal{E}\Big\}.

\bullet

We define

(9.2)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}=(U+V)^{p-1}\pi\left(\frac{f}{(U+V)^{p-1}}\right).

The following lemma is a straightforward consequence of the definition.

Lemma 9.1
.

There exists a unique
\rho\in L^{2}_{(U+V)^{p-1}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
solving the equation

(9.3)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rho+\lambda\rho+p(U+V)^{p-1}\rho+\tilde{f}=0.

Proof.

We know that the operator
T:=\left(\frac{-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda}{(U+V)^{p-1}}\right)^{-1}:L^{2}_{(U+V)^{p-1}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to L^{2}_{(U+V)^{p-1}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is a well defined compact, self-adjoint operator. Therefore, solving (
9.3
) is equivalent to solving
(I-pT)\rho=T\left(\pi\left(\frac{f}{(U+V)^{p-1}}\right)\right).
Since the eigenvalues of
T
restricted to the subspace
\mathcal{E}^{\bot}
are strictly smaller than
\frac{1}{p},
there exists a unique
\rho\in\mathcal{E}^{\bot}
solving (
9.3
).
∎

9.2.
Definition of
u_{R}
and a few straightforward estimates

For simplicity of notations, we omit the subscript
R.
We define

(9.4)

\displaystyle u=U+V+\rho.

Then

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+|u|^{p-1}u=f-\tilde{f}+\mathcal{R}

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=|U+V+\rho|^{p-1}(U+V+\rho)-(U+V)^{p}-p(U+V)^{p-1}\rho.

For
1<p\leq 2,
we have
|\mathcal{R}|\leq|\rho|^{p}
and hence

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}u+\lambda u+|u|^{p-1}u\|_{L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|\rho^{p}\|_{L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|\rho\|^{p}_{L^{p+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}

(9.5)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|\rho\|^{p}_{\lambda}.

Thanks to Lemma
A.5
(b) we note that
\|\rho\|_{\lambda}\approx\|\tilde{f}\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

In section
9.5
we will show that
\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}=o(\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}).
Since
L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\hookrightarrow H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
we deduce

(9.6)

\displaystyle\|\rho\|_{\lambda}\approx\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

However, the estimate
\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\frac{p+1}{p}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}=o(\|f\|_{H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}),
is non-trivial. Poincaré inequality implies
L^{2}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\hookrightarrow H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}),
which creates substantial difficulty in the hyperbolic space. This is where we need a major
diversion in respect of the ideas used in
[
28
]
. We will achieve this in the next two subsections, building upon a delicate improved integrability of the eigenfunctions of the operator
(-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda)/(U+V)^{p-1},
and
improved interaction estimates. The latter one follows the idea of our interaction estimates obtained in section
4.1
and based on the observation that the interaction integral estimates of
U^{\alpha}V^{\beta}
over a half ball can be improved. The former one is much more delicate and would be derived in the next subsection.

9.3.
Improved integrability of the eigenfunctions

We recall that
U=\mathcal{U}[-Re_{1}]
and
V=\mathcal{U}[Re_{1}].
Let
\Psi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
solves the following equation

(9.7)

-\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi\,-\,\lambda\Psi\,=\,\mu(U+V)^{p-1}\Psi,

where
\mu<\frac{p}{1-\varepsilon}
for some
\varepsilon>0,
and we assume
\Psi
is normalized i.e.
\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi^{2}(U+V)^{p-1}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=1.

We emphasize that
\Psi
in (
9.7
) depends on
R.
For simplicity of notations we shall denote it by
\Psi.

The main purpose of this section is to derive the integrability of
\Psi^{2}
with respect to the weight
(U+V)^{-\alpha}
for some
\alpha>0.
Let us state the main result of this subsection.

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
n\geq 3,
and
\Psi
satisfies (
9.7
) and normalized. Then there exists a small
\alpha>0
such that

(9.8)

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\dfrac{\Psi^{2}}{(U+V)^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\,\lesssim\,1,\quad\mbox{as}\ R\rightarrow 1.

The proof of the above proposition requires many intriguing steps. For the reader’s convenience, we break the proof into several small lemmas.

We first collect some straightforward estimates. We remark that Lemma
9.2
(a), and by standard (interior) elliptic estimates,
\Psi
is smooth, and hence pointwise evaluation of
\Psi
is passable.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
n\geq 3,
and
\Psi
satisfies (
9.7
), then the following results hold

(a)

\|\Psi\|^{2}_{\lambda}:=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi|^{2}\,-\,\lambda\Psi^{2}\right)\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\mu,

and

\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim 1.

(b)

|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi|=|\Psi|\left(\lambda+\mu(U+V)^{p-1}\right)\approx|\Psi|.

(c)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\dfrac{|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi+\lambda\Psi|^{2}}{(U+V)^{2(p-1)}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\,=\,\mu\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\lesssim 1.

(d)

For every
0<\alpha\leq\frac{p-1}{2}
the following equality holds

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\dfrac{|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi|^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\dfrac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\,=\,2\alpha\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\dfrac{\Psi\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi,\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W\rangle}{W^{2\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\,+\,\bigO(1),

where we denoted
W=U+V
and
O(1)
is a quantity that stays bounded as
R\uparrow 1.

Proof.

The proof of
(a)-(c)
is straightforward and follows immediately. For part
(d),
multiply (
9.7
) by
\frac{\Psi}{W^{2\alpha}}
and integrate by parts

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Big\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi,\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(\dfrac{\Psi}{W^{2\alpha}}\right)\Big\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\dfrac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\mu\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi^{2}W^{p-1-2\alpha}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\bigO(1).

Now the desired conclusion can be reached after some simple computations.
∎

Remark 9.2
.

Note that in the above we are using
\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}<+\infty
which is not yet justified.
One can proceed as follows: Let
\eta_{k}
be a smooth approximation of the identity as
k\rightarrow\infty
such that
\|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\eta_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\eta_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}=o(1).
Then we can argue as described below (we apply it to
\Psi\eta_{k}
instead of
\Psi
and we let
k\rightarrow\infty
instead of varying
R\uparrow 1
).

Fix
\alpha
small (for the moment we assume
2\alpha<p-1
). Assume on the contrary that
\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\rightarrow\infty
as
R\rightarrow 1.
Let us define the following limits which will play a very essential role in the subsequent proof.

\displaystyle\bullet

\displaystyle\ \ \ l=\limsup_{R\rightarrow 1}\dfrac{\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\dfrac{|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi|^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}}{\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\dfrac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}},\ \ \ \ \ \ m=\limsup_{R\rightarrow 1}\dfrac{\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\dfrac{\Psi\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi,\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W\rangle}{W^{2\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}}{\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\dfrac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}}

\displaystyle\bullet

\displaystyle\ \ \ \mbox{and}\ k=\limsup_{R\rightarrow 1}\dfrac{\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\dfrac{\Psi^{2}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W|^{2}}{W^{2\alpha+2}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}}{\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\dfrac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}}.

Using Lemma
9.2
and the estimate
|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W|=|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}U+\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}V|\lesssim U+V=W,
we conclude that the above limits are finite.
Moreover, from Lemma
9.2
(d) we obtain

l-\lambda=2\alpha m,

and using Cauchy-Schwartz inequality we obtain

m\leq k^{\frac{1}{2}}l^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Lemma 9.3
.

Let
2\alpha<p-1.
Then the following relations hold

(a)

l=\lambda+2\alpha m\geq\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}+\alpha\lambda+\alpha(\alpha+1)k.

(b)

\lambda=l-\alpha\lambda-\alpha(2\alpha+1)k.

Proof.

For
\beta>0
we compute

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(\frac{1}{W^{\beta}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mbox{div}_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(-\beta W^{-\beta-1}\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W\right)

\displaystyle=-\beta W^{-\beta-1}\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W+\beta(\beta+1)W^{-\beta-2}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\beta W^{-\beta-1}(\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}(U+V))+\beta(\beta+1)W^{-\beta-2}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\beta W^{-\beta-1}(-\lambda W-(U^{p}+V^{p}))+\beta(\beta+1)W^{-\beta-2}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W|^{2}

(9.9)

\displaystyle=\lambda\beta W^{-\beta}+\beta W^{-\beta-1}(U^{p}+V^{p})+\beta(\beta+1)W^{-\beta-2}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W|^{2}.

Using Poincaré inequality and (
9.3
) we get

\displaystyle\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(\frac{\Psi}{W^{\alpha}}\right)^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(\frac{\Psi}{W^{\alpha}}\right)\right|^{2}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=-\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi}{W^{\alpha}}\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(\frac{\Psi}{W^{\alpha}}\right)\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi}{W^{\alpha}}\left[\frac{\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi}{W^{\alpha}}+2\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi,\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{1}{W^{\alpha}}\rangle+\Psi\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\left(\frac{1}{W^{\alpha}}\right)\right]\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+2\alpha\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi,\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W\rangle}{W^{2\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{\alpha}}\left[\alpha\lambda W^{-\alpha}+\alpha W^{-\alpha-1}(U^{p}+V^{p})+\alpha(\alpha+1)\frac{|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W|^{2}}{W^{\alpha+2}}\right]\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi(\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi+\lambda\Psi)}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\lambda\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

\displaystyle+2\alpha\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi\langle\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi,\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W\rangle}{W^{2\alpha+1}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\alpha\lambda\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

(9.10)

\displaystyle-\alpha(\alpha+1)\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi^{2}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W|^{2}}{W^{2\alpha+2}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}+\,\bigO(1),

where in the last line we have used the fact that
U^{p}+V^{p}\lesssim W^{p}
and hence
W^{-2\alpha-1}(U^{p}+V^{p})\lesssim W^{p-1-2\alpha}.
In the above expression we divide by

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
and let
R\rightarrow 1,
to complete the proof of part
(a).

To obtain part
(b)
we argue as follows: we multiply (
9.3
), applied for
\beta=2\alpha,
by
\Psi^{2}
and integrate

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi|^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}-\alpha\lambda\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}

(9.11)

\displaystyle-\alpha(2\alpha+1)\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\Psi^{2}|\nabla_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}W|^{2}}{W^{2\alpha+2}}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\,+\,\bigO(1).

Furthermore, using the equation

(9.12)

\frac{-\Psi\Delta_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Psi}{W^{2\alpha}}=\lambda\frac{\Psi^{2}}{W^{2\alpha}}+\Psi^{2}W^{p-1-2\alpha}.

and substituting (
9.12
) into (
9.3
) and by letting
R\rightarrow 1,
we obtain the desired result
(b).
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The next lemma provides us with a control over
k
in terms of
\alpha.
Note that according to our assumption on
p\leq 2
and hence
2\alpha<p-1<1.

Lemma 9.4
.

We have

\frac{\lambda}{1+2\alpha}\leq k\leq\frac{\lambda}{1-2\alpha}.

Proof.

A simple computation using Lemma
9.3
gives us
2m=\frac{l-\lambda}{\alpha}=\lambda+(2\alpha+1)k.
Then

\displaystyle(\lambda+(2\alpha+1)k)^{2}=4m^{2}\leq 4lk=4(\lambda+\alpha\lambda+\alpha(2\alpha+1)k)k.

Therefore
\lambda^{2}\leq(2\alpha+1)(2\alpha-1)k^{2}+2\lambda k
and we obtain the following

(1-4\alpha^{2})k^{2}-2\lambda k+\lambda^{2}\leq 0,

here we note that
2\alpha\leq p-1<1,
provided
p<2.
Hence the result follows immediately.

∎

Now we are ready to prove the proposition
9.1

Proof of Proposition
9.1
.
If the required expression blows up then we have

\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}+\alpha\lambda+\alpha(\alpha+1)k\leq l=\lambda+\alpha\lambda+\alpha(2\alpha+1)k.

The above implies

\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}-\lambda-\alpha^{2}k\leq 0.

This is impossible since,
\lambda<\frac{(n-1)^{2}}{4}
and
k=\lambda(1+\circ(1)),
as
\alpha\rightarrow 0,
hence the proposition follows.

9.4.
Improved interaction estimates

This section is devoted to the improvement of interaction estimates obtained in Section
4
in the sense described below. We know that for
p<2,
there holds

(9.13)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}U^{2}V^{2(p-1)}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\approx e^{-2c(n,\lambda)(p-1)d(-Re_{1},Re_{1})}.

and it is optimal. However, it turns out that we can improve the estimate if we restrict the domain to the half ball
\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}:=\{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}:x_{1}>0\}.

For simplicity of notations, in this subsection, we shall denote
z_{1}=-Re_{1}
and
z_{2}=Re_{1}.

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
U=\mathcal{U}[-Re_{1}]=\mathcal{U}[z_{1}]
and
V=\mathcal{U}[Re_{1}]=\mathcal{U}[z_{2}],
then

(9.14)

\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}}U^{\alpha}V^{\beta(p-1)}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\approx\begin{cases}e^{-\frac{c(n,\lambda)(\alpha+\beta(p-1))}{2}d(z_{1},z_{2})},\quad\ \qquad\mbox{if}\ \ \alpha>\beta(p-1),\\
d(z_{1},z_{2})e^{-c(n,\lambda)\beta(p-1)d(z_{1},z_{2})},\quad\mbox{if}\quad\alpha=\beta(p-1).\end{cases}

Remark 9.3
.

Comparing with (
9.13
) we see that the above proposition is a nontrivial improvement of the previous interaction estimates when
p
is very close to
1.

Proof.

Since
z_{1}=-Re_{1}
and
z_{2}=Re_{1},
it is easy to verify that

\displaystyle d(z_{1},z_{2})=2d(0,z_{2})=2\log\left(\frac{1+R}{1-R}\right).

Therefore
e^{d(z_{1},z_{2})}\approx\frac{1}{(1-R^{2})^{2}}
if
R\approx 1.
For simplicity of notations we denote
c=c(n,\lambda)
then recall that

\displaystyle U(x)\approx e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,z_{1})}\approx\left[\dfrac{(1-R^{2})(1-|x|^{2})}{R^{2}|x-z_{1}^{\star}|^{2}}\right]^{c},

and similarly,

\displaystyle V(x)\approx e^{-c(n,\lambda)d(x,z_{2})}\approx\left[\dfrac{(1-R^{2})(1-|x|^{2})}{R^{2}|x-z_{2}^{\star}|^{2}}\right]^{c}.

We estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{+}}U^{\alpha}V^{\beta(p-1)}\,{\rm d}v_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
